NATIONAL FREE CHURCH COUNCIL.

I-NNI:JAL MEETINGS AT BRADFORD_

ever enterect rite religious . was the ministry of Dr. J. B. Campbell, ot Horton-lane Chapel, who 30 years ago was Chairman of the Congregational Union. A more recent and not less blessed memory is that of the Rev. W. 0. Simpson, the great Wesleyan minister, who was another Peter Mackenzie, and who completely captured the hearts of ' the people of Bradford and of the West Riding. His funeral, attended by a great multitude, was a most striking manifestatios tsf popular affection.

MY DEAR NELLIE, The new spring hats which are now appearing with marvellous rapidity day by day are really charming, each one more perfect than the last. In the matter of taste, beauty, and becomingness the milliners have left us nothing to grumble at this season, for the variety of styles is so great each may please herself as to which she adopts. The all-black picture hat is still in great vogue—larger and more picturesque than ever; but the best are very light in effect. No heavy material is used, black crin, straw lacework, and wired black lace being chiefly employed for the shapes, and, owing to their softness, the brims can be crinkled into all sorts of becoming curves and bends. In nearly all new models a curtain of black lace droops over the brim all round, and in many cases floats as a pendent veil a little to one side behind. But the woman who is not tall and stately is ill-advised if she be persuaded into wearing the large picture hat ; too often does it figure as an object-lesson nii the use and ebuse of things.

In tailor-mades there seems no novelty to be especially chronicled, except it be in sleeves, which in most cases are like the old leg of mutton sleeves, quite tight-fitting on the upper part of the arm, then graduafly widening out into a shaped fulness. This is gained by goring away all gathers, so that the sleeve stands out like an inflated bag, caught in to a narrow band at the wrist, over which is a gauntlet cuff. The backs of skirts at the top seem just as plain and tight-fitting as ever; indeed, rather more so, for there was a ten- dency a little time back towards more fulness hete. However the tailors appear to prefer the plain style, but round the feet the skirt has a very tall fluted appearance, and stretches out to a con- siderable length all round towards the back. There is a good deal of braiding used on tailor-mades; with coarse woollen and linen laces and grass lawn embroidery.

It has long been the ambition of every woman with any pretensions to smartness to possess a black velvet gown, and it will now be more so than ever.• Panne has been tried as a substitute? nut it has not the dignity nor the importance of velvet. It is apt to be meek when it ougkt to be self- assertive. When a man unwarily sets foot upon it, it subsides into such flatness that he thinks it is only the carpet. When he treads on silk velvet he soon discovers his mistake. In fact, velvet has no rival. It is costly, but then it has a long life if properly taken care of; and even the stateliest dame takes care of her velvet gown, though hiding her carefulness beneath a careless air. The day is coming when every bride will have (if able to do her whole duty by herself) in her trousseau a black velvet gown, with two bodices, one for evening wear and one made high to the throat. The two bodices are necessary to the full usefulness of the gown, though the low one may very well be re- placed by a jettod one with velvet sleeves. Just now there is usually a preponderance of black worn, and a black gown lends itself well to modi- fications of colour and embellishment which time and place may demand. A black taffetas frock is an investment to be recommended just now, for it will serve its smart and useful purpose both now and hereafter, and be well in advance of the fashion ; mousseline taffetas is the best kind, soft and subtle and less likely to cut.

The new rosette is or pleated velvet or taffetas, made to lie flat, and sometimes has a smart button to centre it, though where millinery is concerned a knot of little flowers like daisies or violets springs from it. Milliners are now favouring trails of flowers' such as wee pink roses, which they arrange with haphazard grace upon their fur, tulle or straw toques. It is a pretty notion to trail knots of pink rambler roses and tendrils of foliage over a sable turban. A new black straw precisely copies astrachan, and must be looked at twice before one can say whether it is actually skin or not. For Leghorn, a distinct amount of patronage is already secured. Far fewer feathers are being used, and embroidered batiate for tat draperies, with long floating ends, is preferred to either lace or net. Sashes of grass lawn woven with stripes of blos- somed silk are to trim the coarse hats of rustic straw that herald the spring. Some of the daintiest ball-gowns are being made of old-world muslins and batistes of the finest description. The muslin is patterned with small, neat designs, such as were most popular in the early days of our late Queen's reign, and the fashion most adopted is the be-frilled, be-flowered bebe style. Materials will be simpler this spring, and the soft woolly goods thathave been ousted in favourof more elaborate materials are coming back to us. It is good hearing this, and the clever girl will be able to have a great variety of gowns this year if she have a sufficiency of talent to make them up her- self.

Your loving, JEum.

INTERVIEW WITH THE REV. THOMAS

LAW.

The coming meetings of the National Free Church Council at Bradford from March 10 to March 13 are the talk of the Free Churches at present, and with a view to fuller information ' on the subject (writes a correspondent) I sought an interview with the Rev. Thomas Law, the General Secretary, who holds all the strings of the Movement in his hand. "Well," said Mr. Law, briskly," the arrange- ments for the annual meetings are going for- ward splendidly. Our Bradford friends are -.working like Trojans. I held a pastorate in Bradford for several years, and I know the people well. They manage a big affair of this sort well. We shall have considerably over a thousand representatives we have an excel- lent programme, and we shall have the foremost leaders of Nonconformity with us." "But where do these 1000 representatives come from? The North of England and the Midlands, I suppose?" "There you are quite -wrong," replied Mr. Law, with emphasis; "the curious thing is that the Councils in Devon, Cornwall, and other parts of the country remote from Bradford were the first to appoint their representatives. Though our headquarters are in London, our movement has very long tentacles, and our work and in- fluence penetrate to every corner of the country from the Tweed to Land's End. Why, some of the most vigorous Councils are in Devon and Cornwall. Of course, as you know, our move- ment is very strong in the great centres of the Midlands and the North. .And the Councils in these parts will be fully represented." "You mentioned your programme. What is there special about it ?" I timidly inquired. "Special about it," repeated Mr. Law. "What do you mean by 'special'? We have an en- tirely new feature in the mission services, which are to be held in twelve eentres, on the Tues- day evening. Our movement is spiritual and specially evangelistic. The National Council and the local Councils do much work that is not, speaking very. literally, religious, but our spiritual work is first in importance and volume. These twelve mission services will emphasise that fact. The three missioners of the National Council, Gipsy Smith, Mr. W. R. Lane, and the the Rev. J. Tolefree Parr are to take part in them, while the others will be conducted by men so well qualified for this work As Revs. Dr. Horton, S. Chadwick, J. Odell, J. Flanagan, George Jackson, 8.A., W. Y. Fullerton, Thomas Cook, F. B. Meyer, BA., and T. Champness. It will be a great day in Bradford." "Anything else of special interest ?" "Well, it is invidious to particularise, but I think the address of Principal Rainy, of Edin- burgh, at the Communion Service at the close is an item of more than ordinary interest. Dr. Rainy, as you know, is the creator—if any one man can be called the creator—of the recent onion of the Free Church of Scotland and the United Presbyterian Church. The United Free Church, as the new church is called, has a membership of half a million. Dr. Rainy is the first man in Scotland—a great spiritual force, as well as a consummate ecclesiastical statesman. He was anxious to attend our Cardiff meetings last year, but a previous engagement prevented him. He has expressed his delight at the oppor- tunity which he will have of coming into associa- tion with our Movement at Bradford." "And what else would you care to particu- larise in the programme?" I asked Mr. Law. " Well„' you will notice that considerable attention is being given to social questions. Dr. Clifford is to speak on 'The Housing of the Poor,' Mr. J. Compton Rickett, M.P., on The Attitude of Free Church Councils to

Social Problems,' and Mr. W. P. Hartley, J.P., on 'The Use of _Wealth.' The Education Question, which just now is a burning question, is to be discussed by Mr. R. W. Perks, ,M.P., Rev. J. Hirst Hollowell, and Mr. A. E. Hutton. M.P. Other important public matters will come up for consideration. Four sermons are always preached at the Annual Meetings. The preachers this year will be Dr. Robertson Nicoll of the British Weekly, Rev. J. G. Green- hough, M.A., Rev. F. W. Macdonald, and the Rev. C. Silvester Horne, M.A." "What about your Report, Mr. Law ?" "It is a document of 120 pages, containing several new features, and a great amount of detailed, and, as I think, very mteresting infor- mation concerning the work of the National Council and the local Councils. At Leeds, in 1894, there were only 12 Free Church Councils in existence, now there are nearly 800, over 70 new ones having been formed during the past year. A great part of the Report is occupied with an account of our mission work, and I have selected extracts fi om the glowing accounts of the successful work of our missioners which have been sent to the Central Office. Of course, we do much spiritual work other than mission work, such as Parochial Visitation, Mission Van Work, Open-air Services, and Cycling Mission Services. During the year we have inaugurated a great new movement—the Girls' Guild—and, naturally, the Report makes an extended reference to that. Our Movement is rapidly developing abroad—in the United 'States, Australia, South Africa, Jamaica, and on the Continent, and over a dozen pages of my Report are devoted to this aspect of our work. Many Councils and Federations abroad have sought and obtained affiliation with the National Council of England and Wales. The General .Committee has had under considera- tion the question of assisting the Movement Abroad on the lines of the organisation at home. Arrangements will be made for having suitable correspondents in all the chief centres, and it is likely that a deputation will be appointed to visit different parts of the world." "Is the Bradford Council finding accommo- dation for all the 1000 representatives ?" "Yes, the Yorkshire people are hospitable. They delight in work of this sort. Besides, many offers of hospitality have come from members and ministers of the Church of Eng. • land. And at a meeting at Ripon, to be held - in connection with the visit of the National -Council to Bradford, the Dean (Dr. Fremantle) will be one of the speakers." It will be the third time within its brief his- tory that the National Council has met in York- shire. Leeds and Sheffield have already been visited by the Council. It is interesting to note that Bradford has already some very intimate associations with the Movement. The Federation movement is a great stream' and, like other great streams, its sources are to be sought in many springs. But undoubtedly one of the springs was the house-to-house visitation , which was carried out by the Free Churches of Bradford. At the first Free Church Congress, 'held at Manchester in 1892, papers were read by Mr. Percy W. Bunting on 'Town Problems,' and the Rev. Thomas Law on "Nonconformist

Parishes." Mr. Bunting recommended for the purpose of evangelistic social work by the " churches the division of town into districts, worked unitedly, the Nonconformist Council of the town taking supervision of the whole. Mr. Law had been the originator and the moving -spirit of an organisation representing the Free Churches of Bradford, by means of which a house-to-house visitation of the town had been made. ,fflo work the scheme, Bradford was divided mto a large nurrter of sections, and to each church was apportioned a section. Fifteen hundred visitors took part in the work. Three years later, at Birmingham, Mr. Law reported that two-thirds of the Councils had undertaken „honse-to-house visitation. Dr. Berry, the First President of the National Council. was educated at the old Airedale College, Bradford, and preached his first sermon, we believe,'in a neighbouring church. Mr. Greenhough, too, the retiring President, has many intimate associations with Bradford. The Rev. J. H. Jowett, M.A., who was trained at Airedale, was born at Halifax, some 10 miles off. One of the most 'Powerful influ.ences that

IMMERSED 1N LIQUID POISON. imarden Eijr-77: A:Horrible Adventure.

A horrible adventure has recently been recounted by Alexander Robert Heffernan, of 53, Archel-roaC,Fulbam, now an ex-constable of the Metropolitan Police Force, It appears that on the afternoon of February 26th, 1897, he was on duty near the Regents Canal, at a point where the water is rendered highly poisonous by the drainings from chemical and gas works. He was told that a man bad jumped in, to commit suicide. Being a noted swimmer, Heffernan dived into the poisonous water w ithout hesitation. For fully twenty minutes the two men struggled in the water, with the poisonous chemicals bubbling and fizzing around them. Both swallowed a quantity of the poisonous liquid, sad although prevented from Crowning, the would-bo suicide died a few weeks after. ' The effect of the waten • upon the plucky constable was terrible to contemplate The poison, according to the police surgeon, completely destroyed the coating of his stomach. " I was quite unable to digest my food," he says, "and *very time I took any, was sick until it was vomited back. I was placed on the police sick list and sent down to tne Convalescent Home at Brighton twice. After the second visit I had to go back te my own home very bad indeed. I was so weak that when I attemptedl to walk I went blind, and bad to cling to anything near me to prevent me from falling. I bad fearful night- sweats, and would often be seized with a kind of cramp, which seemed to be in my very bones. I could not re- tain food or nourishment, except in minute quantities— just enough to keep roe alive. " I next went into Brcmpton Hospital, but again I derived no benefit. For two years I remained in the police force, suffering in this way, and during the two years I was only able to 'put in three months duty ! Seeing that I was practically done for, in January,lB99, I was given a special pension, and retired from the force. _ _ .

I continued to get worse, despite all efforts. No matter bow tempting and light the food which wee pre- pared, I vomited all I took, and the very sight of food made me nick ! I became reduced in weight from over 13 stone to just over 10 stone, and I was as weak as a kitten. The vomiting, the pain across the stomach and in the back, the sight sweats and the cramping geese- tions continued unabated until September in last year "In that month I was recommended to try some of Chas. Forde's Bile Beans. A few doses made me feel a bit stringer, so I went in for a course, in order te give I them a fair trial. By the time I had taken a few boxes was a different man. The vomiting had stopped, and the dull, aching pain at the pit of the stomach had gone. Every day I got stronger and stronger. The cramp and the night sweats stopped, the specks and flashes before sly eyes disappeared, I became able to walk without needing support, and at night I enjoyed sound, refresh- ing sleep. Te cut a long story short, I aenow quite cured. The effects of the poison have been driven out of my system by the Beans, and my stcmaeh and digestive organs seem to have been re-made. I have eaten so well that 1 have gained twenty-sue pounds in weight, and am still eddieg to it. For the first time for nearly five years, on Christmas Day last I was able to eat and enjoy a good Christmas dinner, "A few months ago I was able to re-start work as attendant in a picture gallery. For three 'weeks before Christmas I was at werk from half-past eight o'clock in the morning to ball-past eleven at night, Saturdays included and I did not fPel fatigued with it " " In my easel Bile Bears seem to have worked a miracle. WARRINGTON TOBACCONISIS AND TRADE DEVELOPMENTS.

Mr Hey, of the United Kingdom Tobacco Dealers' Alliance, addressed a meeting of tobacconists at the Horsemarket-street Cceoa Rooms, Warrington, on Tues- day evening. Mr Charlton, of karlestown, octupied the chair, and amongst others present were Mr Glover, of Chester, and Mr Newton (local secretary). Mr BEY, at the outset, reterred to the annual report of the Alliance, and called attention to its most import- ant paragraph's. Referring to the bonus promised by Mr Duke, be said the bonus was promised verballs ,and it, the Alliance proved itself a strong organization Mr Duke would not stop at one per cent. The apathy of tobacconists towards combination had been the greatest stumbling-block to the progress of the Alliance, but he was glad to say that despite all the difficultiea the Alliance had had to contend with, it was making head- way. The virulence of the attacks upon the Alliance was evidence of its power for an organization that was not a living force was never attacked. The long-expected, long•deferred prospectus of the Im- perial combine was out and truth to tell it was sorry reading for the retailer. The two main points about it for their special consideration were (1) The Salmon and Gluckstein arrangements and (2) The promised bonus. With regard to the first there was no concealing the fact that it bad been received by the trade with mani- fest disappointment. With regard to the promised bonus, ha said if ever it were true that hope long deferred maketh the heart sick.it were true of that long-promised never-seen, indefinable bonus which was buried beneath a mass of figures from which all the skill of the statisti- cal tobacconists could not adduce proofs for even a one per cent hoeus to the retailers and this at a tires when they were calculating profits (on certain lines) at least 71 per cent, more to the retailer than American collateral lines. The bonus would be paid only to such as accepted its provisions, and in his opinion the whole thing was a farce. News was in the aie that a third combine was to be formed. The more the merrier. The retailers stood to gain by , another ring. It woulei be interesting to know why it failed at the first time of asking. He suggested that they should demand (1) that every proprietary line bears its selling price, face value tobe the minimum ; (2) that the retailers have an assurance of their profits for a period of 20 yfi ars whatever befall the Trusts ; (3) that they support and favour only thole lines which tyranted them 20 per cent. In conclusion. Mr HEY spoke upon the value of cohesion, which be said was essential to retailers, if they were to keep the position they held. Mr Gwynn next addressed the meeting and congratu- lated the Warrington branch upon being favoured with a visit by Mr Hey.gie proceeded to speak on the Advan- tages of the Alliance and said be believed it would become a much stronger institution in the near future than it bad been in the pest. Mr 13ey'a services were acknmwledged on the proposi- tion of Mr NEWTON, seconded by the CHAIRMAN. The proceedings then terminated.

Warrington Field Club.

At the ninth meeting of the winter session Mr Charles White presided over a fair gathering, when Mr 6 Carruthers read a paper on "Choice or Chance: A Study in Natural Selection." The lecturer began by citing a number of the Penicuik experiments of Professor Comer Ewert and professed his belief that the influence of a male animal upon a female might betperceived in subssquent progeny of the same female by other males. Despite the destructive criticism to which this belief has been subjected by many competent biologists, notably by Weiss- mann, it is -widely prevalent ; the lecturer did not shrink from reiterating his abiding faith in tele- geny, as it is called, whilst admitting his inability to explain it, He dealt at length with the very obscure problem of the variations which occur amongst plants and avimals,and leaned to the view that. these were due to chance, but that at length after frequent series and at higher levels, somehow choice came in. There was a vigorous discussion in which several members joined, congratulating Mr Carruthers on the amount of work he had put into his paper, but subjecting his theories to very severe criticism.

The criminal business ass concluded at County Limerick Assizes, before Mr Justice Johnson, with the result that in no single case did the Crown obtain a conviction, the juries either rlisagreeing or acquitting the accused. In one case his lordship skid the action of the jury was a regular farce, and he sent them back o reconsider their verdict. Hie lordship said he was tot responsible for the miscarriage of justice, and no Warne attached to himself.

THE BARON'S CHOICE,

ALICE ISAACSON (Author of "After Ten Years," le)

"So the Herr Baron is to marry his, cousin, they say ?" " Whakthe beautiful Fraulein von Tlircke ?" "Beautiful, yes, if you admire black eyes that look as though they could flash out sparks of fire, and hair like the raven's wing, but she would not be my choice were I the Herr Baron, what think yoa, Elsa?"

The girl addressed was leaning with indolent grace against one angle of the low stone wall that divided the garden in which the three friends stood from the. cherry orchard beyond. Her fair hair eeemed touched with a halo of glory, and her eyes, blue as the forget- me-nots in a meadow brook, were gazing dreamily out over the distant scene. Tlie sun was just sink- ing in the western sky, and the forest-covered hills around were bathed in a golden haze, while the per- fume of the flowers was wafted by on every breath of the evening breeze. A long straight path, on either side of which lay fragrant heaps of freshly mown hay, led back to the old verandah-shaded par- sonage. Just below the wall on one side was the high road, which wound uphill and down for miles, away from the little village, past the ancient church, to the nearest town. The larks were murmuring their slumber songs down in the grassy hollows of the orchard, and all the other feathered songsters were twittering soft lullabies among the green leaves overhead; besides, only the musical sound of goat- bells in the distance broke the peaceful stillness that seemed a fitting close to the sweet June day. Gertrud lightly touched the young dreamer's shoulder, and half teasingly with a little moclxing laugh, exclaimed: "Where have your thoughts wandered, liebches, heard you not that we were talking of the I;lcrr Baron"

The three friends in the sunny garden were Ger- trud Hißern, the eldest of the village doctor's numer* -vim family; Frieda Schmidt, the heiress of a well* to-do miller in the neighbourhood, and Elsa Loren% the pastor's only and dearly loved child. The two first were bright honest German maidens full of vitality and fun, but useful in a homely sense rathei than ornamental; Elsa, on the contrary, though one with them in ordinary pursuits was cast in a widely different mould. The beauty of her out- ward person was but an index of the noble spirit within. Often the good Frau Pastor would shako her head with a half suppressed sigh as she watched her child engaged in the daily work of their quiet home , Elsa's loveliness seemed better fitted for a higher sphere, and yet the most menial tasks gained no added grace from her gentle, refining touch. To the old pastor she was the apple of his eye ; he had trained her keen intellect, and watched over and guided her mental growth ; and now this fair sweet blossom that had sprung up from the rugged par- ental stem was the joy and delight of his declining years. What matter though Elsa were lowly born, that her dower would be less than that of all the marriageable maidens around, was she not passing rich in more than wealth could ever buy? The golden treasure of a loving heart, the pure unsullied faith of childlike innocence—the man who ,could win these for himself might be proud of his guerdon etc ever was valiant knight of old; and so the old pas- tor would comfort his wife whenever she confided in him her hopes and fears about the future of their only child. A digression this from the opening of our tale. Elsa turned at her companion's touch, and a slight blush rose in her face as she met Gertrud's laugh- ing eyes. "Pardon me, dear, my thoughts were out over the hills I think, see how lovely they look with the sun- set glow behind them ; our beautiful Alsatian mountains ! I always like to watch them just in this light, but what is it you and Frieda were dis- cussing ?" "Just hear her !" cried Gertrud, "a topic of such absorbing interest and Elsa does not even know what we were talking about." " Ash so, G'ertrnd, don't tease !" it was Frieda Schmidt, a plump, good tempered brunette, who now broke in. Never mind about the hills, Elsa, they look just the same alwayis when the sun shines be- hind them, but tell us, have you heard that Baron von Idedera is betrothed to Fraulein Minna von Tiircke ?"

Again the colour mounted Elsa's cheeks but she answered quietly, "I do not think the betrothal is announced, but it is no secret that the Frau Barman wishes it, Friiulein Minna is very rich." "So is the Herr Baron for that matter," ex- claimed the impulsive Gertrud. " W ell 1 for one would not marry if I were a man, just for the sake of a few more dozen acres of land and an old ruined castle or two unless I really loved somebody." " My father says it is not kind to quet,tion other people's motives when we are not quite sure of them," said Elsa gently," and especially if—" She broke off abruptly; the rapid trotting of a horse had been audible for some moments on the highway, but the three friends hadbeen too absorbed in their conversation to heed it until now when the animal and his rider came suddenly into view. Ths garden of the parsonage was raised above the road. but only so much, as to make it invisible to pedes- trians_; the approaching horseman was on a level with the little group, and as he drew rein and cour- teously raised his hat a conscious look appeared on the faces of Gertrud and Frieda. He was a man of about twenty-eight or thirty, slim and slightly litillt, with hair as fair and eyes as blue as Elsa's own. In- deed there was something akin in the expression of the bright handsome faces that looked towards each other, for though the pleasant " Oaten Abend ;urine "Fraulein " included all three friends, was it merely, fancy that the two pairs of blue eyes met for an int stant andthat when Elsa's drooped beneath their dark- fringed lashes, there was something tenderly signifi- cant in the gaze that rested on her 2 There was no traoe of embarrassment, however, in Elsa's manner as she quietly returned the young man's greeting, but when he had cantered on and was lost to sight by a bend of the road her com- panions eagerly broke silence. "Why, Elsa," exclaimed Gertrud, know the Herr Baron 2"

"do you

.... , " Ach, how handsome he is," murmured Frieda with a sentimental sigh, " Fraurein Minna is a lucky girl." " Of course, Gertrud," replied Elsa;" did you never hear that he was my father's pupil until he went to Heidelberg to study? We do not often see him now, it is true, but he never comes to Ortenach

_ without c,alling upon us." "And yet you did not know that he is to marry his cousin. Well, I should have thought that the Herr Pastor would have been the first to hear such a piece of news as that." "My dear Gertrud, what does it matter? People are not obliged to publish their private affairs to the whole district, and besides it is really no concern of ours who Baron Ton Medem marries. There was a slight tone of impatience in Elsa's pleasant voice. " See," she added, "there is my father on the verandah, he has come to call us in to supper ; now for a race ; which of us three will reach the house first ?" Flinging the challenge at them with a backward glance over her shoulder, she sped off quickly, fol- lowed by Gertrud and Frieda. It was a pretty sight, the three light girlish figures flitting in and out of the cherry-trees and down the broad path sweet-scented from the new mown hay on either side, and the grey haired pastor smiled indulgently even while he shook his head, as Elsa, breathless but triumphant, darted to his side and clasping his area with her hands turned with a merry laugh to await her companions. "Foolish child, what madcap freak is this ?" he said, smoothing the rumpled golden hair with his dis- engaged hand, "thou art too old for such boyish sports." ".Too old to run, father ?" and there was nothing dreamy now in. the expression of Elsa's blue eves as she looked up merrily in the Pastor's face-7" be- cause lam eighteen,—and what is to happen then when the little mother calls out to me Run, Elsa, and fetch thy father's coat and slippers, I see him coming home,' or Elsa run and stop the carrier, he is near our gate, you will just catch him if you hurry.' " The Pastor laughed, he too was used to his wife's energetic injunctions.: " Well, well, saucy one, it is good for the young to be active; and now go, dear child, and help thy mother prepare the supper, while Gertrud and Frieda tell me what you were all three talking about so earnestly down by the garden wall." _

Elsa hurried into the house followed more slowly- by her father and the two girls. Soon the shadows grew deeeper on the garden paths, the birds wen.. hushed to silence, and all things in nature slept; until one by one the silvery stars shone out from the darkening sky and the crescent moon seemed to look down in loving watch upon the quiet home below.

It was the pleasant summer time when we first saw Elsa and her friends standing in the sunlit garden ; 1111 i) W it is autunmy.gad tii.asgteilina_Octobor clag

Down on the plain the grapes are fast ripening for the vintage; there they hang, the deep purple clus- ters half hidden by the yellowing leaves. The squir- rels chatter noisily as they whisk up and down the tali tree-boles, carrying their winter store of nuts; the curious little orange and black lizards have hidden themselves away out of sight in their holes by the forest paths, and the song of the birds is no *longer so joyous and gay as when they sang their %lullabies in June. Once more we find Elsa Lorentz by the old wall she had been busily engaged all the morning in her garden, tying up the tall hollyhocks that were bent by the autumn rains ; gathering the great brown discs of the giant sunflO were that her poultry might fatten on their seeds ; pruning here, plucking there, doing in short whatever she found to be nec- essary ; and now she was resting in her favourite corner, and looking out as before over the peaceful landscape until the clock in the church tower should strike twelve and summon her to the simple mid- day meal. The pastor was writing in his study, the good haus-fiym might be heard in animated dis- cussion with their one servant over some knotty household matter, but here Elsa was alone and un- disturbed ; she had pushed back her shady garden hat from her forehead, and was standing, her hands clasped catelessly before her, on the spot where we saw her first. Again the sound of horses' hoof-beats was heard upon the road and Elsa turning in the direction whence it came, started slightly as the riders appeared in sight. This time it was not a solitary horseman, and one of the two was well known tr, her; the slim fair-haired man we have seen before was sitting over on one side as his horse trotted on, in animated conversation with his com- panion who was looking up intently in his two, but just then he caught sight of the motionlesWgure in the parsonage garden, and suddenly faltered, broke off, half unconsciously tightened his bridle. band, as though he would have stopped, then merely bowed in a friendly manner as they passed. The lady turned also, following the direction of his eyes, and her haughty supercilious stare sent the blood coursing more quickly through Elsa's veins. Even while she met the Baron's look and returned his greeting, she seemed to feel that she was being weighed, measured, found wanting and thrown aside as it were by those flashing black eyes that aps,„ praised her in such ungenerous fashion. For a few moments she stood still, her hands involuntarily tightening their clasp upon each other while her pulse beat more rapidly, her breath came and went in hurried gasps, and her blue eyes filled with tears. A faint impalpable shadow seemed to creep over the landscape and to darken the clear blue sky ; just then the church clock struck the hour of noon, and as the last sound died away Elsa roused herself with a shiver and hastily brushing aside the unshed tears that dimmed her eyes, returned slowly towards the house. Meanwhile the pair rode on in silence. The Baron made no attempt to resume the Conversation which the sight of Elsa had interrupted, though a smile half of annoyance, half of amused expectation hovered on his lips. It was his companion who spoke first. "Who was that girl, Conrad? She was rather pretty." The voice was pleasant and refined but there was a certain ring of determination in it, that showed the speaker was more accustomed to command than to entreat.

" Ah, I wondered how long your feminine car- iosity would hold out, Minna," replied Conrad von Medem; " that pretty girl is Elsa Lorentz, our good pastor's only child." " Oh, Pastor Lorentz' s daughter, and you know her ?" There was a subtle inflexion in her tone that nettled the young man ; he paused before replying -! " You forget, my fair cousin, that although we in our village have not all the advantages of your city life, we are not quite outside the pale of society and are on visiting terms with our neighbours." " We—our --why do you always identify yourself with the villagers and the common people here. Conrad? You are not one of them, though you would make yourself out to be _so." . _ _ .

The'girl spoke impatiently, her dark eyes resting on his face.; he met the look steadily

"Would you have me desert the Schloss then, where most of my mother's life has been spent and to which I shall one day bring home my wife, and-the district where we are known and loved; and would you have me despise the people around because they are of birth not equal to our own ?—Why, old pastor Lorentz taught me nearly all I know and a hard task he had to do it "—he added sotto yore, '• and as for little Elsa "—he broke off with a short laugh— " you must ask my mother to sing Elsa's praisci if you care to hear them,—she has taken an interest in her from a child."

" So, but possibly my aunt would not wish her only son to take quite so keen an interest in Frau- lein Elsa as she does herself." Minna von Tiircko laughed somewhat maliciously, then added— " Ah, Conrad, your face betrays you, but surely you cannot really care for 'this little peasant maiden ?"

For a moment Conrad von Medem hesitated, then looking straight at his cousin said : " Suppose we change the subject, Minna, it is scarcely fitting that we .should discuss Friiulein Lorentz in this manner, and after all my affairs can hardly interest you if the report we have-beard be true—that you are promised in marriage to Count Hochstadt."

The young lady looked slightly disconcerted at this sudden turning of the tables, but though the rich colour deepened beneath her clear olive skin, her eyes met his without faltering : , " Has that on (lit reached you too? Well, and, if it were true what then ?"—her short upper lip curled scornfully—" at least Count Hochstadt is enormously rich, and belongs to one of the oldest families in Germany." " So, and you would sell yourself for rank and fortune to a man old enough to be your father, nay your grandfather. I had almost said. Minna, I de. vontly hope it is not true." Minna von Tiircke did not immediately reply. She turned her head away and was apparently absorbed in the distant landscape ; but if Conrad could have seen the swift softening of the dark eyes and the tender curve of the mobile lips he would have been startled at the sudden transformation of that proud beautiful face, and seeing, might perchance have learnt the secret so carefully guarded that he had never even dreamt of its existence ; but men are ever slow to interpret the subtle changes of a woman's face and rarely seek to understand its cabalistic signs, unless indeed, Love be their teacher. "Should you be sorry then, Conrad, if I marry Count Hochstadt ? " she said at last. The words were calmly almost carelessly spoken, but there was an undertone of passionate vibration, and her heart beat tumultuously as she awaited his reply. " Sorry ! I should think so ! Why, Minna we have always been like brother and sister, you know, and I care too truly for your happiness to wish you to throw it recklessly away." . . _ _

Instinctively her liand tightened on the bridle, and touching her horse's flank with her little gold- mounted whip the animal bounded forward, carrying his rider several lengths ahead. When Baron von Diedem reached her side again the face she turned towards him was haughty and Impassive as ever, and the tones of her clear, well- mod.ulated voice as cold

" Thanks, my cousin, after all your good advice is somewhat premature, and may never be needed, but each one to his taste you know ; if you choose love and a peasant maiden it is just possible I may have a preference for wealth and rank and for the power they bring, without the adjunct of love, which really is quite unnecessary in married life now-a-days. No, do not say any more," she added impetuously, "I am tired of the whole subject ; we had better ride faster ; we are late already and my aunt, as you know, dislikes being kept waiting for luncheon.' Conrad von Medem and Minna von Tiircke had been constant companions in their early childhood ; their mothers were sisters and both were left widows when comparatively young ; but while Frau on Tiircke, whose husband had been a. wealthy banker in Berlin, openly grudged the enforced abstinence from social pleasures to which her deep mourning compelled her, and .was speedily consoled for the death of a man whose money had been his chief re- commendation, Clara von Medem had never re- covered from the shock of that fatal day when the news was brought to Schloss Medemburg that the noble Baron Eberhardt had been mortally wounded while leading on his men to repulse a sortie from beleaguered Paris At first it seemed as if she must sink body and mind beneath the blow that had be- reavpd her of the husband she adored. ; and the little fair-haired son, who looked into her sorrowftn face with blue eyes so like hisfather's that she would turn her own away to hide the fast rising tears. won ilered why the viiitterchen was always sad and never played with him as she used to do; but as time passed on the memory of the father be had lost fading altogether from his childish mind, he was apt to rebel against the perpetual gloom that over- shadowed his home. and to escape from its depress- ing atmosphere as much as possible. But for the 'wise influence of Pastor Lorentz, mother and son liit have Szrovz . houelesslv e it was his

khidly wordirthat first-shintreU trif widowed Ba-toneis the folly of nursing a selfish grief, and that taught Conrad to respect the sorrow he could not under- stand. It was an effort on both sides, however, and Conrad's happiest days were spent with his Aunt Anna in Berlin, where he alternately petted and teased. his cousin Minna who was six years younger than himself, and submitted with fairly good grace to being tyranised over by the little nursery queen. Spoilt as none but an only child could be, Minna von Tiircke from her babyhood was accustomed to have every whim gratified, every wish fulfilled. Her kind-hearted but frivolous mother was proud of the graceful dark-eyed child whose long black curls and brilliant colouring made such a picturesque foil for her own fair if somewhat insipid style of beauty, and so little Minna wasbrought forward on every possible occasion, and those who paid court to the banker's widow found it diplomatic at the same time to praise and flatter the heiress of his wealth. Small won-, den then that the young girl grew up haughty and selfish towards her inferiors, and carelessly indiffer- ent to those of her own rank and station. Her cousin Conrad was the only person who ever dared to tell her of her faults, but though at times they quarrelled furiously, Minna was more gentle and submissive with him than with any other human being, and in her secret heart, though she would have lied rather than confess it, his image was cherished as dearer far than that of the mother who never at- tempted to thwart or control her. Frau von Tiircke rarely visited Schloss Modem- burg; the unbroken monotony of her sister's life was but little to her taste, for the baroness spent her days in strict seclusion with Fraulein Agnes, an elderly spinster of good -family but scanty means, for her sompanion. It was only in the rare intervals when Conrad was it home that there was any approach to social inter- course at the Schloss; then perhaps a few hunting parties were arranged, or a formal dinner given, but only once since Baron Eberhardt's death had the gorgeously decorated state apartments been thrown open, when half the country side had been invited to assist at the grand ball given to celebrate the day on which the young Baron attained his majority. From the time that Conrad'sstudent lite at Heidel- berg began there was a break in the close intercourse betwen the cousins ; his summer vacations were usu- ally spent in foreign travel or in knapsack wander- ings nearer home with student friends; just then, too, Frau von Tiircke discovered it was absolutely nec- essary for her health that she should exchange the winter climate of Berlin for the sunny skies of Italy ; other changes had supervened, so that for consider- ably more than ten years the cousins met but at rare intervals until now, when Minna and her mother had come for a long promised visit to Schloss Medem- berg. The pretty child of twelve had developed into a tall graceful woman of four and twenty, whose regal style of beauty and fascinating manners made her appear older than her years; she had so long been accustomed to move a very queen in society, t 6 receive the homage of the men who composed her mimic court, to be flattered and caressed by all around, that it piqued her not a little to find her cousin Conrad supremely indifferent to her charms. The young baron was altogether a contrast to her ordinary admirers, to the men who, so she declared, had sought her hand, not as the woman, but as the heiress. His easy nonchalance and oierdolence of bearing covered an energy and force of character that impressed Minna strangely in spite of herself, insensibly the strong will that had ruled her as a child regained its power until she found a strange sweet pleasure in yielding to its influence even though she despised herself for doing so. .

Conrad meanwhile was perfectly unconscious of the feelings he hal aroused; that his mother and aunt would approve their union he was fully aware, but even when most attracted by the spell of Minna's imperious beauty, he was secretly repelled by her haughtiness and pride, and involuntarily he would contrast her with a graceful girlish figure, whose loving unselfish nature made her the idol of her bumble home. Not even to himself, however, had Conrad ever acknowledged that little Elsa Lorentz had stolen into his heart untilthat day when Minna's scornful accusation startled him by a sudden revelz- Can of its truth.

:WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Countes3 of Airlie, whose grandmother, Lady Fanny Cowper, was one of Queen Victoria's bridemaids, is attached to the household of the Princess of Wales, and one of the most intimate playmates of little Prince Edward is the Earl of Airlie, who is a great pet both at Sandringham, and at Marlborough House. He is about a year older than Prince Edward, who will be eight next June and all the Earl's brothers and sisters greatly admire his horsemanship. He has three elder sisters, end two younger brothers and alreadylthe youthful Earl displays the 'family trait in a fondness for playing at soldiers. The Ogilvys have been fighters tor genera- tions, and the late Earl. whom Lord Roberts designated "that splendid soldier," was killed in a cavalry charge In the early days of the Boer war. The week-end visit offers an agreeable change to the gaieties of town life, and the Court, and all classes of Society,have adopted the fashion of making short visits into the country. On some occasions the King and Queen are able to combine these visits with their public engagements as was the case on their Majesties recent visit to the West, for the launcaing of the battleship I Ocean, and the laying of the foundation stone of the I Royal Naval College at Dartmouth. ;The King is how- ever an inveterate lover of town life and prefers to spend re ost of his time in London. His Majesty is never so happy as when dining out with his friends in an informal manner, and going on afterwards to the theatre. The Mare very trequently dines out, but dis- likes publicity on these occasions, and, during Lent, the Court will not be seen at the theatres. The American invasion is expected to beat the record this year, and the number of trade; that look to benefit by the influx of American visitcus-during the Corona- tion summer, is legion. The theatres, the hotel keepers, the goldsmiths and jewellers, and tradesmen generally, are all expecting to reap a golden-. harvest this year. Somehow or other Paris has ceased to have the great attraction it formerly bad for Americans, who now spend most of their time over here, in this country. There is however, one feature in our social customs to which Americans never get used. In their own country the typical American hostess is always fussing and bustling about with a set determination that her visitors 'shall enjoy themselves. Eve over here the guests are left to entertain themselves and though this is sometimes charming, and %voids any strain on either hesteas or guest, it often appears cold and surprising to thelstranger. In one of the American States, it bas been ruled that an action for breach of promise of marriage, lies against the estate, of a deceased fiancé. In a case recently be- fere the Courts of Indiana, a young woman sued the executors of the man to whom she was engaged, and recovered a substantial sum for breach of contract. It is to be presumed that the poor man could not help dying, and if such a law were to become general, it would add a new terror to matrimonial engagements, against which an engaged couple could only provide by insuring their lives. An entertaining exhibition devoted to women's sphere in life, is to be held at the Hotel de Ville in Paris, dur- ing the coming summer. The object is not only to illustrate every department of home life, but also to show the woman at work in the various industriesovith a view to securing better conditions for women in the shops, and manufactories. There will also be a Congress to discuss woman's position in the world, so that those who are not merely interested in the mechanical appli- ances of vanity and usefulness, may listen to feminiee oratory upon those aecial questions and evils, for which humanity has not yet succeeded in finding .a remedy. So far we have never had a really comprehensive woman's exhibition, but the French are peculiarly suc- cessful in organising such displays, and in all proba- bility there will be much to be seen and heard at the Hotel de Ville, that will be specially interesting to women. Of dressy headgear we have an almost too abundant choice ; for morning wear and for the athletic girl there is less variety. The sailor shape is hopelessly overdone, and the Panama is the only real substitute. But a real Panama is a thing of price. It is delicate- in colour, seft as silk, will roll up into the pocket, and endure being sat upon uninjured, also, it lasts a life-time. Now, for one seassee's wear, we see on every side the cheap substitute, yellow in hue, and firm of outline. It is no doubt useful, but it is too frequent in its appearance, and a meeting between one of these moderns and a real Panama, is humiliating for the wearer el the former. Far better are the large tricorns hats, modified of shape, tipped forward over the eyes in true French fashion, and simply trimmed with soft silk and quills. In these cycling and tennis are possible and pleasurable; se Silk coats still obtain—indeed it seems to be essential that every wardrobe should contain a; least one of them . —and various are the makes of silk pressed into this service, quite irrespective of the gown below. There are taffetas, and glacet. also a sort of mixture of these two !makes called taffetas-elect% and all the host of brocleSa and le; osa dee. Semi-long are the majority sf these coats, some made in the prevailing Louis XV. style. with deep lapels, packets, and cuffs. These are all too frequently frilled, pleated, tucked, cut away and beset with insertion, bed;zened with jEt, steel, gilt, and other gleaming beads, or, loaded with silk pessementerie Mere successful, from an artistic point of view, are those coats which, built of rich material, boast moderate additions in the way of trimmings. The fash'enable handkerchief C has almost reached vani‘hing point, and this state of affairs is probably due to the disappearance of pockets. Numbers of women stow their hanokerchief in their sleeve, their belt, the pouched front of a blouse, and other convenient corners but to achieve this end gracefully, the handkerchief must be of almost microscopic proportions. The coloured varieties have teen popular during the past winter but the best dressed women are never to be drawn from , their allegisnce to the handkerchief of snow-white hue: Arrinle as the skirts are which, appear :in " ready- made " establishments they seem not ample enough for the ornamentation that is crowded on to them. Large geometrical devices, formed by strappings are tbere also eassementeries, mighty of scroll, outlined with jet, sequins, and othet gauds: Then the lace appliqués, These seem to grow larger every day. The wise among us will avoid even the least ereamented (?) among these skirts and instead build, or have built for us perfectly plain well fitting specimens, for the house and outdoor wear. There is a good deal of probability in the suggestion that people suffering from asthma or bronchial troubles may derive benefit by keeping a small quantity of coal tar in their bedrooms. The odour may not be agreeable but there is no doubt that the gases given off have a soothing effect upon the lungs. There is an old saying that anyone with a weak chest or inclined to pulmonary disease, should live near a tan yard, cr gas works. The spicy breezes from the tan pits may not he very pleas- ant, but tt eir therapeutic qualities are beyond question and everyone knows that the gasworks are regarded as a first class health resort for children suffering frorn whooping-cough. Three of Helen Mather' e well known novels, " A Man of To-day," "The Juggler and the Soul," and " The Sin of Hagar " have lately been taken over by Mr John Long and in future they will be published by him. "A Man of To-day "is now in preparation for issue in six- penny form, and this will be warmly welcomed by ell who are not yet acquainted with what has proved to be one of Helen Mather's most charming books. A LIVERPOOL WOMAN IN T ROUBLE. Remarkable Affair in Edge-lane.

Investigation has recently been made into the rather remarkable iociiient concerning a Liverpool woman, which has attained some publicity in the Edge—lane district of the city. The matter is full of interest for itself alone, but it becomes more important inasmuch as it is not an isolated instance by any means, but one typical of the state of things among women in that and other parts of Oa city. The affair relates to Mrs Sarah Ann Webster, a woman of forty-one years of age, who resides at 508, Edge-lane, Liverpool.

MRS. WEBSTER (from a photograph.) Mrs. Webster stated to a reporter that her troubles commenced three years ago. "1 was taken," she says, " with pains in my stomach and across my body. I had to go about the kitchen stoopinge—l couldn't stand up straight. I was that way for months, and it is only through taking Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people that I sin strong and well as you see me to-day. I first called in a doctor, who ordered me to bed, and I was in Led three days. It turned out to be ulceration of the coating of the stomach. I could not keep anything on my stomach. It all came back, and I was that weak through lack of nourishment that I couldn't lift my hand up to my mouth. Indeed it would be difficult te exaggerate the suffering. I exe perienced, until by good fortune I learned the merits of Dr Williams' pink pills for pale people. The pain I suffered before I knew of them mulct be described. I was under the doctor uutil I was sick and tired of going and I have a cupboard lull of empty medicine bottles- They eased me for a time, you know, and then I was as bad as ever. The disease,called in medical language gastric ulcer, arises out of neglected indigestion. Every time I ate anything I suffered intense pain until it came bock,aed then'l would be as hungry as ever. But in time even hunger deserted me,Lr I became very weak and ill through lack of sufficient nourishment, and did not feel like eating. Of course," Mrs Webster went on, " my weakmess got much greater, and, indeed, after a time I couldn't do my little bit of work, and for three years this went on ; it was perfectly miserable for me and everybody." "Mow did It stop ? Well, I'll tell you. One day, about six months ago,one of my sots read in the paper about a cure by Dr Williams pink pills for pale people. It referred to a person who bad the same ailment as myself. My husband said to me, 'lt will never break us ; we will get a bog of Dr Williams' pink pills and try them.' Tie got a box and after I had taken four Pills I found great relief. I kept on, and I got altogether seven boxes of Dr Williams' pink pills, and the last box is only half empty. Dr Williams' pine pills acted in A marvellous way on my stomach. They seemed to heat the ulcerated coating, which formerly rejected all food, and as time went on I could take a stronger and stronger diet, without suffering those bad pains. They gave me a good appetite. I can now eat my food and enjoy it, the pain has gone onmrelY, and now lam as well as ever I was in my life. " Yes, I really believe, in fact Pm quite sure, that Dr Williams' pink pills cured me, and nothing else. I would not be without a box of them in the house for anything. No, I don't at all mind you publishing what I say. Dr Williams' pink pills have done me good, and my experience may be of benefit to others."

, N

Of late the various members of the,Royatfamily, from His Majesty downwards, have been very busy visiting various parts of England and officiating at different social ceremonies, and gn Wednesday last the Prince of Wales was present at Manchester to open formally the new building, named Whitworth Hall, in connection with Owen's College. This addition, which has been in the process of being built for a long time, is a very fine adjunct to the College buildings, which it, so to speak, completes by filling up the south-east angle of the bite. On its north side stands the large tower which has for many years been the principal feature of the College, while on, the west. it touches the Christie Library, which was, until the erection of Whitworth Hall, the newest portion of the Owen's buildings. It is bounded upon two sides by important thoroughfares having Oxford Street on the east and Burlington thoroughfares, on the south. On the around floor of Whitworth Hall are a number of comparatively small rooms, about eighteen feet square, which will be used for various purposes, and include a kitchen, which will be useful in the serving of any banquets in the Hall PROF. HOPKINSON, THE PRINCIPAL OF OWENS COLLEGE.

• WHITWORTH HALL THE IMPOSING ADDITION TO OWEN'S COLLEGE, MAN- CHESTER, OPENED BY THE PRINCE OF WALES THIS WEEK. above. This hall is a very fine room, 130 feet long by fifty wide, with a stage at the north end, surmounted by galleries and a magnifi- cent organ, the gift of an anonymous donor. it is roofed with oak on the hammer beam principle, somewhat modified, and the roof is sustained by buttresses and six great granite columns. The Prince of Wales was reCeived by Professor Hopkinson, the Principal of Owen's College, and others of the leading lights, and opened the building with a very neat and complimentary speech. His Royal Highness was also entertained 'to luncheon by the Lord Mayor and Corporation. As these lines are penned Society is agog with excitement in anticipation of • the 'list Drawing Room or Court of King Edward VII. and the Queen Alexandra, which, being solely by invitation issued directly by Lord Clarendon, the Lord Cham- berlain, will naturally be a very brilliant and select affair, to which access has not been so easily gained as is only too common to most functions of London Society in the Twentieth Century. The interest is greatly . added to by the 'fact that this first Court of the new King and his Consort marks a definite change in the order of Royal ceremonials from that which has been followed for THEIR MAJESTY'S FIRST more than three- COURT : THE EARL OF CLAREN- quarters of a cen- DON, THE LORD CHAMBER- tury, as not since LAIN. Queen Charlotte passed away have the official . presentations of ladies been made to their Majesties in the evening. The hot Drawing Rooms of the late Queen Victoria, with theii long periods of waiting at uncomfortable times in dresses more suited for the evening than the afternoon, were not popular either with dowagers or debutantes, and the reversion to the old order of things has been hailed with great delight. In addition' there is no doubt that such functions look far better and more natu- ral by artificial light than in the glare of the afternoon. The first of the Courts of King Edward and Queen Alexandra will be a particu- larly gorgeous affair, as their Majesties have made a great point that all possible members of the Royal Family at present in England shall attend, with the result that the circle round the Throne will be the most notable seen for many• a long year, while the Court for the first time really goes out of half-mourning. Her Majesty will wear a dress of the palest yellow, and some very rare jewels which have not been seen in public for years. It also marks the beginning of what is likely to be a very brilliant round of functions and festivities in the Coronation season, which may be said practically to commence with this Court at Buckingham Palace, though there will be a short • abeyance at Easter, when the King will go to the Riviera and her Majesty will be in Denmark. It will be remembered that, upon acquiring a fresh fortune' the financier put aside a quarter of a million for the ruined shareholders of the Union, of which only about one-third was claimed. Another serious charge which Mr. Iless preferred was that Mr. Bottomley had endeavoured to obtain election to Parliament in the South Hackney division by open bribery, and that he had paid the local landlords to supply voters with free refresh- ments. Altogether, the libel complained of was most generously all-embracing, and was evidently ,designed to drive Mr. Bottomley once and for all out of public life and the realms of finance. The former is best known as the practical founder of our now considerable stretch of Empire in West Africa, and has held the post of Governor of Nigeria since its cession to the Crown by the erstwhile Company. Originally a GEN. SIR FREDERICK LIIGARD.

37 INTERESTING ENGAGEMENT. soldier without fortune, if not actually a soldier of fortune, Sir Frederick Lugard, more by chance than anything else, struck West Africa and made it his caraping,ground for many years, gradually working up his reputation until his is now one of the best-known names in the Empire, and -he ranks as both military and civil founder of the large confines of Nigeria with its hinterland and neighbouring territories. In the days when he threw in his part and lot with the West Coast of Africa, it was looked upon. as the white man's grave, and still is, though to a somewhat lesser extent, but hi a health stood the climate, and materially aided his mental and military abilities in making his career so successful. His bride-elect, Miss Flora Shaw, has for many years been at the head of the Colonial department of the Times, and written much in luminous and lucid style which has greatly developed the public knowledge of our fellow-subjects across the seas. She has held special commissions from the Times, in Australia Canada, Klondyke and South Africa, and by each has added to her name. The last named is the one in which she came most within the eye of the whole world, as she was out in Cape Colony' at the time of the Jameson Raid and, being in the confidence of the conspirators, was of invaluable service to her paper. She was one of the leading persons in the events which led up to the Raid, and had to give evidence before the Commission on the subject, in which her name was frequently men- tioned. The engagement is a widely popular one, and Sir Frederick Lugard and Miss Shaw will be wished a long and happy married life by the whole Empire. Mr. Brodrick made a sweeping condemnation of the whole Volunteer Service, in a speech delivered last week before the HouQe of Commons. Naturally there was a deal of protest from several members, who are themselves volunteers, but it re- mained for Col. Sir Howard Vincent to prove that Britain owed a debt of gratitude

to the Volunteers which she will find it hard to

In a vigorous and lucid speech Sir Howard Vincent contended that Mr. Brodrick's re- marks were totally unjustifiable. He denied that the Volunteers had laid claim to a position to which they were not entitled. He showed that although the standard of musketry had constantly increased, the facilities for shooting have absolutely diminished. He criticised the Duke of Bedford's statement that the country had a right to demand an efficient force of volunteers in return for its expenditure, and he proved that as a force the Volunteers is the cheapest in the world. It costs the ratepayer only £3 10s. per man per head, whereas the regular soldier costs £lOO, the militiaman £B, and the Imperial Yeoman £39. The Volunteer Force was raised solely for home defence, yet in 1899 it was the first to offer its services to the field force, and in j'anuary, 1900, it gave 10,787 men to the regular 'Army. In 1901 it gave another. 5,985 inen and 5,000 to the Imperial Yeomanry. Considering these facts and the knowledge that the Volunteers labour under innumerable disadvantages, it will be seen that the country is receiving very good value for the money it expends on the Force. vVARR)NGTON_INFIRMARY AND

DISPENSARY.

Report and Accounts.

The report and accounte of the Warrington, Infirmary and Dispensary for the year 1901 have jest been published. The Board of Management in their report state that they are pleased to 'be able to stale that whilst the ex- penditure has only increased to the extent of £l3 53 9d, the income bag increased to the exteet of £644 7a 9d. This is mainly accounted for by the special efforts made by the Workingmen's Cominittse in promoting a cycle parade ard carnival, Increased income derived trom the purchase of the leasehold property adjoining the Institu- tion, and an increase in the congregational collections. ' Toe net rental derived from the leasehold property yields an income of nearly four per cent on the purchase price, as against three per cent received from Lanca- shire and Yorkshue Railway Debenture Stock. The adverse balance brought forward from last year of £1,140 17. 81 has been reduced to £563 3s 2d. The Beard appeals to the friends of the institution to wipe out this adverse balance. There has been a decrease in the number of patients treated in the house of 168 as compared with the year 1900; the average number of beds cceupied being 2948, and toe average cost per bed, after allowing the usual Is 6d per head for out-patients and 4s per head for home patteuts, was £7O Us 61 as against £59 lie lid in the precediog year. The Board beg to acknowledge an intimation from the Executors ot the 1 ite Mr John Crosfield, who was a trustee and be governor, and whose death is much regretted, that he has bequeathed a legacy of £l,OOO, free of duty, to the institution. They have also to record with regret the death of Mr Charles John Holmes, who was a life governor, and of Mr Robert Davies, who was a tutee and life governor. Mr G P Dewhurst, of Oughtrington Park, becomes a life governor in respect of a gift of £25. MEDICAL BOARD'S FIRST ANNITAI REPORT. fal"The increase in size, importance, and work of the Warrington Infirmary has of late years pointed to the advisability of submittiag to the governors of the hospital an annual report on the general conditions of its working and prospects from the Honorary Medical Staff in addition to the more sietalled etetiatica furnished in the report of the Resident Medical Officers. This course hap ticeo followed with advantage mother hos- pitals and the staff have great pleasure in submitting this the first report to you. In the outset we beg to congratulate the hospital on the smoothness of the wcrk- ing, and of the cordial relations which now and always have existed between the Board of Management and the Medical executive. This i 3.11 subject of congratula- tion in that it secures the most efficient application of the:hospital funds for the charitable purposes for which they were designed. Before commenting on the statistics of our wcrk a word is needed as to the members of our staff. The lamented death of Mr J U Oornall deprived us cf a comrade and the hospital of one whose name had been moat honourably associated with it for very many ears. Mr Charles White has resigned his post of honorary consultant and with it his ee sition on the staff. His prolonged association with the Warringten Infirre- ary is so writ known to all that it needs no setting forth bore. The intense and active interest he has always taken in is well-b4ng will, we feel sure be retained in spite of his retirement. His services to the hospital on the Board of Management have been as assiduous as those in his more special department, viz, Chairman of the Medical Committee. In this latter office his advice and direction on the committee have been as conspicu- ously valuable as his dicta at our medical consultations and it was with the unfeigned regret el every member of 3 oer staff that the anneuncement of his resignation of office was received. The resignation of Mr J Guest Oornall, rendered compulsory by his appointment to the important post of /Medical Officer of Health, whilst depriving the hospital of his valuable assittince on staff, will, we are glad to know, retain his services for the good of his native town. On the resident staff, Messrs Battle and Unripe resigned office in pursuance of their medical careers elsewhere. Whilst recognising and cordially acknow- ledging their good work we congratulate ourselves on securing in their plebe the efficient services of Messrs More and Ferguson. As to the hospital ststistics. On three occasions some of tile wards have bad to be kept empty for some time swing to the outbreak of infectious disease (measles and scarlet f-ver.) This not only has decreased the active usefulncite of these wards but again strongly emphasises the growing necessity for the provision of isolation wards which'do not at present exist. The recent out- break of smallpox and possibility of an epidemic of this disease also points imperatively in the same direction. Partly owing to this compulsory closure of the wards, the hospital has been able to accommodate fewer cases than in the preceding year. Another factor is that the number of patients who wer3 received for shore periods —of a few days only—has been necessarily diminished, a fact aft Penne the mere, aggregate number of in-patients to a considerable extent. On the other hand the num- ber of out-patients, home-patients, and accidents, has shown a very considerable increase, showing on the whole the ever-enlarging work of the Infirmary. It is a subj-ct of great moment to point out that con- siderable additions will be needed to the accommodation and equipment to bring the efficiency of the hospital into line with that of similar institutions. The provision of a Roentgen apparatus is almost sine quci non, and with the laying do ern of the electric phut in the town should be easily attainable. The treatment of skin and other diseases by specially devised apparatus is every -day marking its value, and the necessity of providing hospitals with them. The possibility of the value of similar appliances in the treatment of cancer is of great importance to Warringtonitself a wall-marked centre ef this terrible disease. The present accommodation for the nursing staff in the hospital can only be described as wretchedly inade- quate and inconvenient. We would suggest to the Governors the extreme urgency of the provision of a properly-constituted Nurses' Home, both for the effici- ency of the hospital itself and as a means of supplying nurses for private cases in this district—a great desideratum. The hospital lift for 'conveyance of patients to the different floors of the building and to and from the operating theatre is cumbrous and clumsy, and quite inadequate to its requirements. The provision of an elactrio or other lift is in our opinion urgently indicated. We recognise that these recommendations contemplate a heavy outlay, but we anneal with con- fidence to our Board of Management—an-el through them to our Governors and the Warrington public—for the provision cf means to carry them out. They are abso- lutely necessary to maintain the cherished efficiency of the Warrington Infirmary, which hat alwaye hitherto received the energetic support of the Warrington pub- lie.—E. A Fox, G W Joseph, C E Richmond, Ernest E Bowden."

NOUSE SURGEON'S REPORT.

1111Dr7 H Mort. in his report,states that during the year 1901, 456 patients were admitted to the infirmary, of whoin 290 were males and 166 females. This shows a decrease of 168 as cowered with the year 1900. Of three 370 were surgical, and 86 medical cases. The surgical cases include 202 admitted as accidents. 33 deaths have oeeurred In the institution during the year, 23 as the result of accidents and 10 from disease. Many of these patients were adinitted in a moribund condi- tion, 14 dying within 24 hours of admission. A Cup-Tie Forecast. • The "Yorkshire Post" says That hardy annual, the Northern Union Cup Competition, will spring suddenly into prominence on l6atarday next. To trace the probable course of the tournament, to forecast the winners of the various rounds right up to the final itself, is an amusement popular for the nonce among the football community concerned. As in the old Yorkshire Coallenge Cup Competition —which still has its enthusiastic adherentiareduced in numbers though they be—it is possible to pre- pare a complete chart of the draw before a match is played. In this the tournament differs from that of the Football Association Cup,wherein each draw is made separately, at a cost of much incon- venience and uncertainty. Many Associationists have advocated the adoption of the Yorkshire Challenge Cup system for toe Football AssJciation Cup, but the Association managers prefer their own and old-established procedure. A draw of GI clubs works out into four sections of 16 each, and the best—or should it not be the most fortunate ?—cub in each 16 will appear in the semi-final round. To discover the probable quartet to reach the penultimate stage of the Northern Union tournament is the object of this article. Toe respective " squares " may be dealt with in the order in which the Cup circle is evolved from the complicated and unsymmetrical official het. -a Cup Committees never have an eye to the Euclidical art. Tbey draw their rough problems from the hat, and leave the newspaper man to do the rest. Briefly stated, the chief clubs in the first of the four squares are Rochdale Hornets, Huddersfield, Millorn, Leeds, Dewsbury, Manningham, and Salford. Hudders- field and Leeds aught to meet at Faxt)wn in Round 3, and tile winner should have Salford at home in Round 4, Salferd's only difficult task up to this point being at either Dewsbury or Manningnam—probably at Dews- bury. Leeds folk are confident of having the fourth round at Efeadiugley,but success in a (nip tie is not easy at Fartown, as we think Loeds and Saltord will find out in turn in Rounds 3 and 4. which are the only rounds Huddersfield can have at home. For the present, Viers- fore, Huddersfield can b 3 written down as the first of the semi-finalists. In the next section, the constituents include Batley, Warrington, Bradford, Leigh, Runcorn, and Oldham, together with Brighouse Rangers, Barrow, South Slaieldif, Castleford, Bramley, and ether impossible Cup winners. The crucial match in this section will prob- ably be Batley v Bradford in Round 3. and it is the writer's opinion that the winner of that tie will take part in the dual, Bradford's task at Warrington in Round 2 promises .to be ve y difficult—they found a similar task there too difficult last year—but they should accomt lish it this time, and if so, there will be a tremen- dous battls the following week at Mount Pleasant. It may be accepted es a fact that the Batley team them- selves are looking forward to Bradford's visit with keen z,st, and that the Bradfordians would also like to have toe opportunity of regaining a lost lauret or two in the engagement. It is a very awkward tio t ) forecast, but past experience of Batley v Bradtord Cup bat:les, extending back to the mid bO'a, justifies the nomination of Batley as tueprobab!e winner, should the foitbal Fates belni' the dabs together. The third" square "probably contains the next cup- holders. Broughton Rengers, Hull Kingston Rovers, Hull. Halifax, and Swinton crate the list, and of thme the Northern Rugby League champions hold the advant- age of meeting their most formidable rivals—the Hull clubs, Halifax, Or Swinton—it home. Swinton's severe downfall at Betley last Saurday probably concealed the tnam's real programme against Halifax this week-end. Halifax followers think their team can succes4 ally pull the " Lion's" tail, nor is this thought at all unreason- able. The probabilities are, however, that it is Halifax, and not Swinton, who will be called upon to visit Broughton in Round 4. A WELL-KNOWN YORKSHIRE COUNCILLOR.

MR. COUNCILLOR CHESTERFIELD, for fifteen years member of the Corporation of Rotherham, has not many years to live before be can count seven decades, but he is, considering his age, a healthy and hearty-looking man. He has a thriving business, is very popular, a thoroughly good business man, and is noted for quiet consistent work rather than for "gallery talk " at the Council meetings. .

But no public man can do his duty if he has not good health. Councillor Chesterfield has had several visitations of that dreadful scourge influenza, but in the spring of 1899 his illness took a severer turn than usual, and left behind it some serious after-effects.

"Whenever I stooped," said Councillor Chesterfield, "I had great difficulty in rising again, and I suffered severe pains in the back, which I put down to cold having

===== 0002601_19020314_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
SETTLED IN MY KIDNEYS.

I also had severe rheumatic pains in my shoulder and down ,the arm to the elbow. I took what I considered a common-sense view of the matter. Having read in the paper an article on the curative properties of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, I procured a box of the pills. By thus treating the complaint in time I found a 5. -.y remedy, and prevented what might have . - a long illness. Before I had taken one box of the pills the pain in my shoulder disappeared altogether, and has

===== 0002601_19020314_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
NEVER RETURNED SINCE.

DEATH OF MRS. F. MONKS.

IVarrington people heard with sincere regret on Monday of the death of Mrs F Monks, wife of Mr F Monks J.P., of Walton Old Hall, who passed away at Smedley's Hydro, :klat- , lock, at an earls hour that morning. Tht,4 deceased lady, who was widely esteemed for her many estimable qualities,bad suffered from heart disease for a considerable time, and about a fortnight ago, not feeling well she decided to visit Matlock, where she hoped to recuperate. The end was quite unexpected, and when the sad news was made known in Warrington on Monday it came as a great shock to the relatives and friends of the deceased lady. The deepest sympathy is expressed for the husband and family in the irreparable loss they have sustained. Mrs Monks bad interested herself for many years in most of the local philan- thropic institutions, and her services on behalf of the Infirmary, of which she was one of the Ladies' Committee, and the Warrington and District Nurs- ing Association were given readily and ungrudg- ingly. Her loss will probably be most severely felt at Cairo-street Chapel and schools, which owe much ta her willing assistance in many ways. The body was cremated at Manchester on Wed- nesday, and on the following morning, the urn con- taining the ashes was deposited in the family grave connected with Cairn-street burial ground. The proceedings were of a strictly private character, only the relatives being pre.ieut to witness the interment.
