DEAFNESS AND CHRONIC DRY CATARRH OF THE EAR.

Deafness caused by dry Catairh of the Ear, is so uni- versally regarded as a hopeless condition that it is a source of great satisfaction to know that the Drouet treatment has been wonderfully successful in this class of cases. The proof of this is be found in the February •• Supplement to the Journal for the deaf," which con- tains reports of a number of cured cases. A free copy ,of this publication can be obtained by addressing the Secretary of toe Drouet Institute, Regent,'s Park•road, , London, N.W. One of the most remarkable oases is that of Mrs Moat, and her cure, in view of her age (.78) and of the stubborn character of her Deafness, shows that the methods advocated at the Drouet Institute are so suc- cessful as to give the greatest hope to those whose de- fects of hearing have been pronouLced incurable. This patient expresses her satisfaction in havieg regained her hearing, as follows :- 21. Strawberry Bank, Dundee, Jan. 17th, 1902. Dear Sir,—Being in perftct health,and my hearing so much improved that I can hear distinctly anyone speaking from a platform. I feel in need of no further ttlitment.—Thanking you, I am, sincerely yours, Mrs MUAT.

Those who wish advice as to the application ot the Drouet treatment for any special case, should write for a Report Form, which enables anyone suffering from deafness, or any ear, nose and throat disease, to submit their case to the Consulting Physician. This Form is sent with the Journal and Supplement, and advice by correspondonce 18 always given free of charge.

THE REDISTRIBUTiON PROBLEM.

Representation of Cheshire and

Lancashire.

Anomalies to be Remedied.

The recent speech of Mr Balfour on Redistribution not only commits the Government to the intrnduction of a Bill during the present Parliament, but also indic- ates the lines on which it will be drawn. The history and character of constituencies, as well as the popula- tion, will be taken into account, and there will be as little disturbance as possible of the continuity ot our present system of representatios. It may be assumed therefore, that the coming bill will be based upon the precedents of 1832,1867, and 1885, in depriving A certain number of small constituencies of direct representation, and giving the seats so lib.rated to overgrown county divisions and boroughs. TJ enable our readers Co see at a glaoc2 tee anom dies of our representative system, we give in the f allowing table the population totals and averages for each portion of the United Kingdom, and Also for Cheshire, Lancashire, Yorkshire, the metro- politan constituencies, and selected groups of fifty of the largest and fifty of the sinallest ersastituencies Average Populttion Name. Seats. Population. per Member. Raglan 1 Wales Scotland Ireland lOl ... 4,455,546 ... 44,124 Lancashire Yorkshire Cheshire London Largest Constituencies 5) ... 5,963,60 ... 119,210 Smallest Constituencies 53 ... 1,128,325 ... 22,5E3 •-

NOTE.—Uttiversity members are not included in the above figures. Woolwich and West Ham are reckoned as London Constitu sillies. The last two groups it:while constituen- cies in each of the three Kingdoms. It is scarcely necessary to point out that the interest of the above table centres in the last column, which snows the average population per member. The aver- age for the United Kingdom is 62,715,but it will be teen that in the fifty smallest constituencies it is only 22,566, while in the fifty largest constituencies it is no less than 119,210. It is foreign to our present purpose to discuss the question of Irish representation, bat the statistical side of the argument is forcibly set forth in the stove table. The County of Chester is represented by thirteen members five of whom sit for borougns, and eight for County divisions. In the following table we give the population for each constituency, and also the number of voters on this year's register : Boroughs Population Electors. Birkenhead Stockport (2 seats) 78.871 ..................12.551 Stalybridge Chester County Divisions Wirrall Altrinoham 78,796 Crewe 74.545 13orthwich 60,C99 Hyde 60.931 Knutaford 63,199 ltddisbury Macclesfield 51.700 The average peoulation per memner for the Cheshire boroughs is 56,512 and of electors 8,645. The oversee population for the eight county divisions is 68,799, ani Of electors 12,076. Owing to the overlapping of Lanes shire into Cheshire constituencies there is a total norm lation in the boroughs and county divisions of 832,951 as compared with 814,555 within the county boundaries In the last Redistribution Act b3roughs with less than 15,000 population were merged in county divisions, and in view of the growth ot the population during the Is.st two decades it is expected that the minimum for consti- tuencies will he raised in the coming Bill to 25,000 or 30,000. In 1885, two-member constituencies of less than 50,000 inhabitants were semi-disfranchised ; pro- bably the line will be drawn in the Government Bill at 70,000. In this ease Stockport will narrowly escape the loss of one of its members. Birki n'sead is one of the filty seats in the group of the la,gest constituencies and has an irresistible claim to a second member. Of the county divisions Wirral heads lh3 list with 100,830, while Eddisbury and Macclesfield are somewhat below the general average. Under a redistribution scheme there will be some modification ef the boundaries of the county divisions to render the population more equal, but as the average population of the eight divisions is only 6,000 above the standard for the king- dom there is not likely to be any addition to the representation. The net result of redistribution then, so far as Cheshire is concerned, will be the addition of one seat at Birkenhead and the re-arrangement of the county divisions. THERE'S NOTHING HALF SO SWEET IN LIFE AS LOVE'S YOUNG DREAM.—So sang Tom Moore years ago, and everyone who bail been fortunate enough to consummate a happy engagement by a suitable marriage will, doubtless, cordially endorse .this statement. What could be pleasanter than to be the proud possessor of a nice home, presided over by a loving, winsome wife, whose sole desire is to make things comfortable for you and the little ones—when they arrive? Especially do you realise the charm when you know that, thanks to your own Industry and the economical inethod9 of the aforesaid good lady, the "nest egg" at the bank is steadily augmenting.

MR. .1311 N MORLEY in Mr. Morley and the Government. his survey of the politi-

_ _ ..... cal situation at Man- chester enjoyed one advantage in common with Lord ROSEBERY when the latter spoke at Chester- field. Both these statesmen have in the last year enjoyed a measure of retirement, anct they have been saved from the antipathies which are excited by frequent'' speeches in a period of national stress and irritation, It is due to that*

both, however, to say that neither has con- cealed his views. Circumstances made theta spectators instead of active fighters in the arena, but when the occasion seemed to demand they have never hesitated to speak. One thing is certain. Whoever else may regret that Mr.Moki.Er devoted so much of his speech to criticism of Lord RC6E•• BERY, the Liberal Imperialists cannot complain on that score. They claim the right of indepen- dence, and even of freedom from official leadership while still of the fold, and they will concede the same recognition of the right of the other extreme. It is a pity thdt their controversies continue ; but as Sir HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN said the other night, they are happily conducted without personal difierences, and, in fact, in perfect good temper. In his wider review of the events of the past !couple of years Mr. MORLEY made what is the prime indictment against the Government. They have failed in ordinary foresight, and so it is that ,mblic confidence cannot help being shaken. The story is one long tale of miscal- culations, and of failure to appreciate the situa- tion. When diplomacy was carried to its extreme and a high line was taken, the Government did not contemplate for a moment that the Boers in- tended to fight. Otherwise the negotiations would have been accompanied by an accumulation of troops within our own territory. We know now perfectly well that our original garrison could have been swept into the sea. When troops were being poured into the country and the horrors of war had begun, the same lack of appreciation was shown as to the hospital arrangements,and enteric did the mischief which Sir WALTER FOSTER foretold. it would unless special precautions against fever were taken. No reasonable person believes but that the Goverunient wished to do its best for the houseless refugees whom it placed in Concen- tration Camps, but there again ordinary care was lacking, and the camps became abodes of death to the very young. Over and above these things was the constant failure all through this aliair to understand the temperament of the enemy. Some of the proclamations were merely foolish ; others were essentially mischiev one. The public execution of Boer commandants whatever their misconduct, was an error of judg- ment, for it excited the sentiment of the • Dutch people in the Colony, and added A hundreds and hundreds) of recruits to the enemy. r As for the computsion upon the relatives of con- ~ damned men to attend and witness their execu- tion, Mr IVIORLEY rightly characterised it as an. "infernal atrocity." Most of these facts are 4, becoming all too apparent now. The difficulty in - speaking of them is that they discredit our coun- i I try, and every patriot shrinks from that. At the same time it is neccssary by criticism of the Government to point out to the country the dangers which the situation involves. Mr MouLEf spoke out strongly, and remembering all that 1135 happened, there need be no wonder at the shar " ness of his rebuke.

able disaster to Lord .11Erno EN. Ic was, however, an assembly of curious and sympathetic, rather '1 than anxious, legislators. That was demonstrated clearly enough in the melting away of the crowd after questions, although such pertinent questions as the Army and Navy votes were the orders of the day. It was a day when, if any keen interest were taken in military affairs, there would have been profitable discussion since the subjects arose that have commanded wide attention, notably the vote touching on rifle ranges. There was some mild criticism as to the slow progress of the War Office in meeting the convenience of Volunteers and rifle clubs in this matter, but Lord STANLEY'S equally mild explanation was readily acceptal,and no one seems in a hurry about the matter. Lord STANLEY'S statement was to the effect that nego- tiations for land look a considerable tirne,anki that the Government was doing what it could with a fitting regard to economy. The fact, however, remains that the deficiency is felt in some districts of scanty popoulation, where agricultural land runs close up to town and to railwaylstations. If the country were really eager to embark on Interprises which might involve us in conflicts with foreign Powers, its representatives in the House of Commons would be a good deal more concerned than they are at present as to national defence, for that, after all,must be the thought of any people who desired to go in for an assertive Imperial policy. Despite all the recent discussion about volunteer equipment, it is tolerably evident that the Government take a very cool view of the situationAand members of the House generally held to the desirability of jogging on quietly. Our militarism, so far as it affects the mass of the population, is not so ardent even as that of the little 14, Tublic of ',Switzerland, which re- quires nearly all its capable citizens to approve themselves in the handling of rifles. The War Office would be wiser if jit put efficiency before economy in this matter, and hastened some- -what the acquisition of fresh ranges. But many people who arenervous as to the effect of recent events on our national policy will be relieved to find what restraint, we might almost say com- parative indifference, is shown on this subject. The money for enlarging the Navy is voted readily, but the thought in its concession is primarily for the maintenance oZ our commerce; there is no suggestion of:using our maritime force to strike a blow at any one. The re-organisation of the Army is an obvious necessity in order to keep up the recruiting, but then again our attitude is rather to maintain our position than to do any- thing bellicose. Much has been made of the feverish ebullitions of the crowds on .lafeking Day, but it was a passing outburst of feeling, and is not likely to be repeated. There is nothing to justify the apprehensions that the country as a whole favoursJingoism, although it is clearly the , duty of an Opposition to watch expenditure into which the present Government, Wong in majority, may drift. •

A young American girl is at the present moment setting a very difficult problem to the authorities in England by whom matters of Court etiquette are determined. Miss Alice Roosevelt, the daughter of the President of the United States, desires to be present at the Coronation, and the official world is racked to devise some method by 'which she can be invited, and given for the occa- sion a status which shall not be resented as a slight by the whole womanhood of the United States. To the unobservant critic of affairs it might seem perfectly easy to recognise this bright and clever young lady as of equal rank with the foreign princesses who will be in England. i She s the daughter of one of the most powerful rulers in the world, and, as such, might seem to claim at least as much recognition as the daughter of a European sovereign prince. On the other hand, the chil- dren of Presidents of Rr.-3publics have always been regarded in their own countries as ordinary , civilians ; and in America, of all lands, they have 1 taken special pride in parading as such. How then, can they expect to be received in foreign countries with all the honours due to princes who have behind them the glories of heredity. Presi- dent Roosevelt is naturally not very pleased at the idea of his daughter visitiag England as merely "an American girl travelling privately in the household of a special ambassador." Still, the difficulty in conferring definite rank on so interest- ing a visitor, great as it is' 'cannot be.insuperable. 34 is to be hoped away will be found, and shortly. It would not be agreeable to see the problem first solved in a visit by this represeutative of young American womanhood to the German Court instead of our own.

••• - ••••••••.••••••••• The British disaster in the Western Transvaal has drawn attention to the peculiar position in Which Lord Methuen was placed by his military chiefs. Lord Methuen, Lieutenant-General in the British Army, late in command of the First Divi- sion of the First Army Corps,was relegated to the Position of a mere column commander chasing the Beers with a few hundred mounted men. It is obvious that the importance of this reverse arises not from any effect it will have on the course of the war—tor it no more changes Lord Kitchener's plans than did •I'vreefontein, Vlakfontein, or the More recent convoy disaster near Klerksdorp ; but from the fact that the General of the British troops was one who had held high c9mmand, and still occupied a position of eminence, in the British Army. For some time among military men there has been discussion as to the propriety of allowing Lord Methuen to occupy the position which he did, which was practically that of a Brigadier- General; and as it became evident how easily a column might be surprised by a stronger force and the officers captured, the feeling grew that Lord Methuen should either be given a proper head- quarters and staff, and the position of Lieutenant- General in command of the Western District of the Transvaal, or be allowed to come home for the rest which by his activity and energy he had fully earned. The disaster which was feared has now occurred, and Lord Methuen is in the hands of the Boers. Had he been a mere column com- mander the importance of the capture would have been greatly minimised; but there is no question that it will now constitute a very valuable encour- agement to the Boers who are fighting,

lIIMINimam.

MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter.

Parliamentary business is said to be in such a state as to almost preclude the possibility of The Budget being introduced before Easter. This will, otcourse, allow amateur Chancellors of the Ex- chequer all the more time to prepare their schemes and proposals for submission to Sir Michael Hicks- Beach. To the present moment the best effort in this direction, perhaps, i, the suggestion of a clever and ingenious correspondent to tax meta- phors. These " would afford an excellent form of indirect taxation. They are not necessaries, and,as a rule, are only used by the well-to-do. Possibly a graduated scale might be drawn up imposing heavier duties on special forms of metaphor, as, e.g., those drawn from Scripture. I sometimes fancy myself living in Old Testament times, when I can- not got the most trifling information on any politi- cal question without corning across phylacteries, tabernacles, ephode, and the like." A truly nice idea this, if it were not in danger of being com- plicated by the recent decision that the author of a speech is not the man who makes it, but the one who reports it. But,if the Chancellor of the Ex- chequer should shrink from this, let him turn to another branch of literature and make a writer of verse take out a poetic licence. If he would only hurry up now,and impose an especially heavy duty on Coronation Odes— not Oaths this time—he would have the heartfelt blessings of men who already think of the Coronation witu apprehension, dreading its effect on minor poets.

In every part of the provinces the public have been reminded that last week was the week of "self-denial " on the part of the members of the Salvation Army. What a change has come over the "Army" within the past twenty years! From being regarded with a kind of pious horror by regular church and chapel goers,who were not infrequently disturbed at, their devotions by brazen instruments and that dreaded big drum, the Hallelujah lads and lasses have taken to churches and chapels of their own, and some of them very imposing buildings, and have become quite as " respectable "as their neighbours. Indeed, "the man in the street" no longer regards them as intruders and " a nuisance," bat with perfect toleration and even respect. All of which, of course, is as it should be. It is really wonderful that the Army reduced by its decidedly crest leader, " General" Booth, to a state of proper discipline, has been able to accomplish so much as it has through the self-denial practised by its mem- bers. The self-denial week is a great object lesson for the whole nation, and has once again been loyally and very generally obierved in the pro- vinces. In these days of prevalent and increasing desire for comforts, and even luxuries on the part of the working classes, the example set by the Salvation Army is most creditable, and should not be slightly regarded.

What to do with their street arahs, is a question that has been forced upon the attention of local authorities, at one time or another and in one form or another, in most parts of the provinces as long as most of us can remember. In soca of our larger towns they appear to be regarded philosophically, as R kind of necessary evil best left alone. This, indeed, may have been the case at Birmingham until the other day, when a delegation waited upon tho Watch Committee with a request that it would bring its authority to bear as against street trading by juveniles. The delegates there, like other people elsewhere, hug the delusion that a better regula- tion of the streets by the police would get rid en- tirely of the " street arab" nuisance, and that by some system of licensing they might not only clear the thoroughfares of youthful loafers and beggars, but that the latter would by the same act be swept wholesale, as it were, out of the streets and roads into suitable situations, where, of course,they would be kept under proper and salutary control. It is a c mvenient kind of delusion. For the strong pro- bability is that the greater the number of children prevented hawking matches and newspapers in the streets the greater would be the number of idle loungers in the courts and alleys, beguiling the time with pitch and toss, fisticuffs, and other juvenile diversims. It is in idleness that most of those very habits are acquired which so often frintify in crime, and any material reduction, therefore, in the number of the street vendors of the younger sort, besides indicting great hardship on the parents and fami- lies of the children concerned, would almost cer- tainly entail a serious addition to the criminal population The proposal to give into the custody of the police any child seen' offering a box of matches or a newspaper for 'sale without a licence, is one that working people can never be brought in this country to tolerate. But this does not imply that there is no room or need for some better way of regulating juvenile trading in the streets. Suit- able moans of a less drastic tendency might be devised if the authorities concerned woull only take the needed trouble.

" Accommodation for Man and Beast," is a not unfamiliar phrase as heard in its relation to the conveniencesl of the wayside inn, but the public have not yet been made fully aware, probably, of how much or bow little it may mean when inter- preted under differing circumstances, They are brought a step nearer to the right conclusion, how- ever, by the recent decision of Justices Dar- ling and Channel! on the important point --from the benighted wayfarer's point of view—of when "wine inn" may be declared by the "unaccommodating" host to be "full." An auto- mobilist whose machine had broken down, found himself in want of a night's "accommodation " at Honey in Surrey, where the landlord of the village inn said he could not oblige him as his hou,e was full. The traveller was willing to sleep in the bar- parlour, the smokeroom, or to put up anywhere,but the answer was the same. A claim for damages ensued, and after the case had gone from one court to another, it came before the Judges named at the Queen's Bench, where it was finally decided that a licensed house is full when all the available bed- rooms are occupied. This may be a sound adminis- tration of the lavv,and the law itself may not be the " bass " which one of Shakespeare's characters dubbed it, and as some other peoele have some- times considered it,but in this case it clearly leaves something to be desired from the " weary way far- er's "standpoint, and has a distinct tendency to spoil the poetry of the " taking mine ease at mire inn" reflection, while Shenstone's traveller will no longer need " Sigh to think he still has found The warmest welcome at an inn." \

" Nor, when as Phoebus with his fiery waine, • Unto his inns began to draw apace," (as set forth in the "Faory Queen ") he never could have seen the legend," Beds engaged ; house full," or, if he had, he would have declared it another gool and substantial reason for abolishing " ions business "altogether as "a delusion and a snare."

One of our greatest experts in the sugar bidustry fears, strange to say, that the iimmediate effect of the abolition of the sugar bounties in the few sugar refineries that exist in Great Britain will be disas- trous. " The bounties are not be alnlished till September, 1903. Now, what will happen in the meantime is that this country will be swamped with bounty-fed sugar. At present there is a sur- plus stock on the Cmtinent, estimated,at 1,200,000 tons. All this, and the sugar produced between now and September let. 1903, will be thrown on to the English market. During the present year one can calculate that the •foreign refiner will import into Great Britain 200,000 tons above the actual rate of consumption, and as much more as he can manage to manufacture" If this estimate is correct the Chancellor of the Exchequer and the English consumers will alike benefit, but Mr Sigmund Stein, of Liverpool, fears that there will be a very bad time indeed for the English refiners and the West Indian growers until September, 1903. After that,. la?weverollthere is to be a good time for both, and it is of interest to agriculturists to know Oat he suggests, that beet growing will be worth their attention. They have heard something of this latter I kind before, of course, but that is another story.

At Chester Assizes on Tuesday. before Mr Justice Walton, Harry Caffrey, for stealing a shirt and neckerchief at Runcorn, on the 4th January, was sent to gaol for four calendar months' hard labour. His Lordship said as far as he knew prisoner had not been in trouble before 1895. Since that time, however, there had been a great number of small sentences passed on him. He had not the slightest doubt the best thing to do would be to have him kept in confinement for a considerable length of time, but he did hope prisoner would try to recover himself and he would be dealt with leniently con- sidering his bad record. 421 SPECIAL MErriNer or THE URBAN COUNCIL. REPORT OF THE MEDICAL OFFICER FOR THE RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT.

Dr Adams M.S., C.M., has just published his annual report on the sanitary condition ot the RuneAm Rural District for the year ending 1901.—We take the follow- ing extracts from the report :

===== 0002601_19020314_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
AREA COVERED .BY THE COUNCIL. •

The Runcorn Rural District comprises tne whole of the Runcorn Union with the exception of the Runcorn Urban Districts and comprises for y townships.

POPULATION OF THE DISTRICT.

The:district area comprises 49,309 acres. In the 1891 census, the population was 24,665 ; and in 1902, 23,203. —lt will be observed that the census population has, in the ten years 1891-1901. diminished by 1,462 ; and that this diminution is observed in the Runcorn Regis- tration Sub-district to amount to about 820 ;—in the Frodsham Sub-district, to about 336 ;—while in the Budworth Sub-district, excluding Stockton Heath,there Ins been a decrease of 61; bu,; as Stockton Heath alone has increased by 928, there is a gain in this Sub- district of about 287. Of the 30 townships in the dis- trict, only twelve have increased in population during the last ten years, while 28 have decreased. The increased populations are entirely confined to the resi- dential (as opposed to agricultural) t ownships, such as Helsby, (400 increase) Sfockton Heath, (900 increase) and Walton Inferior, (300 Meccas-) •, while the most marked decrease has occurred in Frodsham,(nearly 600) Froth ham Lordship, (about 100) Halton, (over 300) and Weston, (about 260).

RIM HS &ND DEATHS. INTERESTING STATISTICS.

During the veer ending December 31st, 1901, 634 births, and 312 deaths, were registered as having oc- curred wilain the district ; there ie a decrease of 27 births on the ten years' averege, and a decrease of 67 deaths on the same period. The number of deaths for 1901 is the lowest number recorded during the las; ten years,aud the death-rate is ! 1.6 per 1,000 below toe aversge of the last ten years. !Of this corrected total of 300 deaths, belonging !to your own district, 2 were " not certifled,"— while 21 tormed the subjeot of Csreners' inquiries. Of the 634 births, 318 ;were males, and 316 females, a difference of only two bet wen the sexes ; last year the ! difference was only one. Of tee total reeistered 312 ' deaths, 165 were males,and 147 were females. The num- ber of births however, is the greatest since 1896. I hope ! this upward trend in the birth-rate will be maintained ! in subsequent years. THE SMALL-Pox EPIDEMIC AND THE HOSPITAL AT 10011 E. , W•tti this disesse occurring in so many parts of the ennury, it is not surprising tnat it made its appearance , among us during 1901. Infection appear +to have been derived from the neighbourhood of Liverpool Docks. , The Small-pox Hospital at Moore was ready for the reception of patients, but as this patient could be cern. p'etely isolated at borne, a large country house, the hospital was not opened. Every person on the premises where the dis 'age broke out, WM promptly re-vaccina- ted, and no extension of the di-eese took place. Toe hcspital is quite ready for the reception of patients, but I wish to draw your attention to the need of a water-supply to the Hospital ; Since the Ship Canal authorities removed their water tank which supplied the Hospital, all the water for the steam disinfector has either to be carried, or rain-water collected from the roof of the Hospital. An extension of the Warrington Water Works main in Moas-lane wou'd be the most satisfactory supply, and could be carried out for a moderate annual charge ; the opening of the Hospital under existing conditions would be carried out under very considerable difficulty, as all the drinking water would require to be carried a distance of nearly a quarter of a mile. I hope your Council will put the Hospital on an efficient water supply at as early a date as possible, in view of the probability of requiring to open it for patients in the present year. THE NEED OF AN ISOLATION H.;SPITAL. In close connection with this disease is the question of an isolation hospital. [am glad to be able to report that since last year this matter has been advanced a further stage towards accomplishment. A site has been selected and approved in the township of Dutton, less than half a mile from the Union Workhouse, in a most desirable and suitable locality ; terms for purchase of the site have been acreed upon, and I believe ths trans for of the and to the District Council will shortly be effected. N thing will teerefore remain,af ter considera - Elm of ways and means, but to proceed with plans, tenders,and building of an isolation hospital during the present year, and I sincerely trust that the end of 1902 will see the building in an advanced stage of con- struction.

DIPHTHERIA.

This disease has been verp prevalent in 1901, no I^sia than 44 cases having been notified,resulting in 8 deaths. The chief: outbreaks were in Frodsham, where 30 cases occurred, with 6 deaths, and in Ttteloran. where 6 cases occurred with 2 deaths. The disease abated after clos- ing the schools, but recurred in . few weeks,and casual cages were still occurring at the close of the year. It is probable that defective water supply is the main cense of the disease in Thetwall during the last two years, and not, as frequently alleged, the so-called ' Tannery nuisance.'

DEATH AND FUNERAL 0 F CAPTAIN SAVAGE.

With startling suddenness, the death occurred on Tuesday morning, of Captain George Savage, at the advanced age ot 72 years. Tie deceased gentleman was chairman of the Zillah Shippinp Company, and was to be seen attending to busines as usual on Sat- urday. He was not well at the time, and on the following day he became so much worse that it was fouud necessary to send for Dr Naden, his medical attendant. There was no improvement on Monday and he had a rest!ess night. 0.. t an early hour on Tuesday morning he came downstairs for breakfast and was sitting in an easy chair near the fire,when he suddenly expired. The cause of death was li:ntrt disease.

The late Captain Savage was born in the year 1830 in Runcorn, and early in life he engaged in the coasting trade. When 18 years!of age he removed to Sankey Bridges and resided in that district until he was 21 years of age, when he married. He actively identified him- self with religions institutions in the distric', and his early removal to Widnes was regretted. lie was a man of no mean intelligence and whilst at Widnes he was a successful local preachers In a short time, however, Mr John Cros- field invited him to return to Warrington and take charge of the Workingmen's Mis- sion at Bank Quay as pastor. The request was acceded to and for three years Mr Savage devoted himself to the work which flourished exceedingly in his hands. At the end of three years, Mr Savage resigned the position—a position be had held with honour to himself and for the benefit of those with whom he came in contact. As a token of respect, the congregation and school presented him with an illuminated address, a timepiece and vases. Immediately afterwards, Mr Savage entered into business as a draper and general dealer in Liverpool road and carried it on successfully until 13 years ago. About this time, he decided to give up the business and resume his active connection With the coasting' trade. A coaster named " The Zillah " was ulti- mately bought and this was the first vessel owned by the now well-known Zillah Slipping Company Limited. From small beginnings, with Captain Savage as chairman and Captain William Savage as managing director,the company has now half-a- dozen vessels and has become one of the best known coasting concerns trading on the west coast. Captain Savage identified himself with Bewsey- road Wesleyan Chapel, but he freely gave his ser- vice sto numerous religious bodies. For many years he interested himself in the work at the Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Chapel. Be was one of the founders of this Chapel and took part in the cottage services which preceded it erection. At the time of his death he minted a class in the Sun- day school at Bold-street. He gave freely of his time and money for the furtherance of Christian work and his death causes a vacancy in local Weeleyamsm which it will be difficult to fill. He was especially fond of children and numbers made him their confidante and adviser.

COWARDLY ASSAULT ON AN EARLESTOWN POLICEMAN.

At Newton Petty Sessions on Thursday, Henry Bailey ,was charged wit n being drunk and dis- orderly and with assaulting Police-constable Mellor. The officer said that on Monday last prisoner was drunk and disorderly, and on the way to the police station became very violent, and kicked him on the right leg several times breaking the skin. For the assault on the police defendant was fined 20s and Costs, and 53 for being drunk, or twenty-one days in default.

THE THELWALL NUISANCE. - •

'Mr John Lothiby asiestant siocretary to the Local Government Board wrot,, inquiring what progress the District Council had now made in piping the ditches referred to therein. He forwarded at the same time extracts from a further letter which the Board had re- ceived from Mrs Quennell. The letter was as follow!: —Cuerden Place, Waste-lane, Thd wall, Warrington.

( Sir, I beg to acknowledge the receipt of your letter and many thanks for your information eoncerning this Tannery Brook nuisance. I should have acknowledged it before but owing to ill-health I have been unable to do so. I was pleased when I read the report of the Runcorn Rural District Council, that they had decided to pipe and cover over the brook. I thought then they would carry it out without any further delay. After their decision they did start to do something at it but they stopped as suddenly as they began, so that there is no sign whatever of improvement at preseut I am sorry to say. The stench from the brook at times is quite as ha I if not worse than ever. One of the nights list week, also last night when my husband was returning trona his work the smells were so bad as to cause him to vomit. Ile is obliged to pass the brook or go quite a mile and a half out of hi,. 'way. I was very much surprised when I read Mr Ashton's letter contradicting my statement as to the brook smell- ing. On the day that my two children passel it previ- ous to their illness it did sin- I! and badly too. If Mr Ashton was there on that day they were not there at the same time—between 8 and 9 a in, and it was some con- siderable tim3 after before the members of the Council visited the tannery, and no doubt the smell had some- what cleared away by then. Tnev say it never smells whea they come, only an ordinary tannery smell. Was it an ordinary tannery smell, we could put up with it, and not trouble you with our continuous comeleints. I only wish they had to be close to and endure the smells when hey are at the worst for one day only—whet we people tot Waste-lane have had to endure for long weeks and months. They would then understand why we are anxious to have something done in the matter. There is always someone ill in the neighbourhood. Two of my near neighbours are ill now and have been in bed weeks, and the doctor that attended them said that their systems were thoroughly poisoned. Does there need any further proof than this to convince you that the smell from that brook must be poisonous and dan- gerous to human life. I think it cruel to treat a matter so indifferently that C&USC3 SO much sickness, disease and expense, as poor people like we are in Waste-lane, with large families, really cannot aff Jed to pay large doctor's bill, and have the necessaries of life, out of a workingman'a wage. I sincerely tru t you will, if possible, do something for us in the matter." _ . . .

The -Clerk informed the Council that he had acquainted the Local Government Board:with what they were doing in the matter.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.] Ratepayers interested in local affairs will have noticed from the report of the last Council meeting that there is a vacancy in Mersey Ward owing to the retirement of Mr Grice. This vacsncy will have to be filled up, it is believed by April sth. It is stated on the best authority that already one gentleman, Mr A It Norman,wh 3 has already gained many honours from the electorate has intimated his willingness to stand for the vacancy. Su far it is impossible to say whether there are other local gentlemen desirous of being numbered among the city fathers. The probability is :that there will be another walk over which speaks ill for the interest ratepayers have in local affairs. Somehow or other it is absolutely impassible ta convince the man in the street that municipal legislation is far more important to him as an individual than im- perial politics. Assuredly Runcorn is a silent, yet eloquent witness to the indifference of the people to local government.

The half-yearly estimates fer the Boardiof Guar- dians are now published, and ratepayers will be pleased to learn that there is a slight reduction compared with the corresponding period last year. This satisfactory feature is, however, more than counterbalanced by the estimates of the Rural Council, which show that there is an advance of nearly one penny in the compared with the pre- vious estimates. Naturally the already burdened taxpayer will grumble, but he should find consola- tion in the thought, like Captain Marryat's hero, that 'things might be worse." There is no doult that the Council in season and out of season prac- tice the most rigid economy consistent with effi- ciency.

In a most polite letter the Water Committee of the Urban Council have informed tne:District.Coun- cil that they are qlite satisfied with the result of the recent analysis of Runcorn Water. Facilities will, however, be given to the Distric,, Council to make further tests if necesiary. As the Rural authority are not at all satisfied,Dlr Farrington,the inspector, has b een deputed to make the necessary tests. What the conclusion of the whole matter will be i 3 impossible to say but there iq evidently a little irritation at present between the two local authorities.

From Weston has been lodged rather a serious complaint against the Urban Council. A short time ago a water main burst and after the necessary repairs had been executed the road was left in such a shocking condition that a furry with horse attached sunk inithe ground and was with diffi- culty extricated. The Rural Council have, however taken the matter in hand and the Urban Council is to be requestsd to attend to the repairs.

Mr Walker, of Warrington, was responsible for a rather interesting debate at the emincil meeting In passing a fence bndering a footpath in the Council's district, the gentleman in question tore his coat to the value of .C 1 15s, and now claims that amount from the Council as the authority re- sponsible tor the condition oC the road. The fence, from inquiries made by the surveyor,belongs to the Ship Canal Company, and the question arises whether the owner is liable or the local authority for not having previously taken steps to obviate a serious danger to pedestrians. Realizing the im- prance of the problem, the Council have charac- teristically deferred its solution pending additional information. The public is seriously affected by the Council's decision for it will, of course, be a prece- dent in determining complaints of a similar nature, which may occur in the future.

' Many a man has lived to regret giving his honest convictions to a world wherein lurks the renown of ridicule and criticism And judging by the ser- mons of the Rev IL B Blogg who lately was under- stood to advocate Sunday sports and the continen- tal Sunday,fthe reverend gentlemen lives at present in this regrettable state ot mind. Since his remark- able utterance at the Rural Deanery conference, which raised the vicar into an unenviable, unex- pected, and unwished for notoriety,the district from Widnes to Helsby has been in a state of ferment. Every post, I am informed, brings shoals of letters reproaching, con- demning, some applauding him for his action. His parishioners have so exercised themselves over the matter that the Vicar deemed it incumbent to defend his views from his pulpit at Overton Church. His morning sermon, listened to with breathless interest,lwas an eloquent one. And if rounded periods and plausible argument could carry conviction the congregation could have almost said with Festus, "Verily ! thou almost persuadeth us to be Christian on your lines." But the fact re- mains that the sermon will add fuel to the flame already kindled and instead of allaying the parochial mind will perturb it still more. The onus was thrown entirely on the reporters, who, he asserted, only gave a garbled and condense 1 a - count. This statement is essentially misleading. Condensed the reports had necessarily to be, but as the reports were copied oft tne reverend gentle- man's M.S, and it was impossible for the alleged mis- takes to have been made, in common fairness to the pressmen Mr Blogg ought to specify the parti- cular passages which were wrongly reported and not make general and ambiguous charges.

FOOTBALL.

Runcorn did very -I;liatTdraw at Salford on Saturday. The game was an exciting one through- out and though there was none of that scientifiC display which delights the heart of the football critic there was nevertheless some first class sport witnessed. There were absentees on both sides so neither can claim to have laboured under any unfair disadvantage in this respect. The visitors had un- questionably the best of matters and but for pure ill luck would have certainly lowered the colours of the "Reds." Salford invariably lost ground in the exchanges which were 'too prominent a fea- ture. Richardson and Jolley did not open out the !game as much as they might have done and repeatedly skied the ball instead of setting the three-quarter line into motion. The forwards:too, of which Moulton, Walker and Langley were the pick, outshone their vis-a-vis and had by far the best of matters. The most prominent feature of the game was the heartiness of the tackling, It was a game of' pulling don" and scoring victory by an opponent's downfall. The defence of course outshone the attack and these tactics may account for the pointless draw.—Houghton played a sterl- ing game and saved defeat repeatedly. NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

By " Ovalist."

IWarrington's last league match but one was played 1 on Saturday and resulted in a comfortable win over the Hunslet team, who took two points out of Warringtoa earlier in the season. They brought a full team down, and with Warrington suffering very much from ab- sentees, the odds before the match were considerably in their favour. The luck of the toss was also with thern,but the victory accrued to Warrington,principally because the home team played with plenty of dash and the substitutes rendered a good account of themselves.

Playing with the wind in their favour the Hunslet men soon had Warrington in difficulties, but their scoring powers were apparently only moderate—always excepting that wonderful goal-droppist, Albert Gold- thorpe. Early he took a penalty and popped the ball over from rather long range in a nonchalent manner winch was rather annoying to the Warringtonians.Con- sequently they made things hum in the direction of the Hunslet goal-line, Fish and Harris both having very hard tuck in not scoring. Then the Goldthorpe Com- pany—not limited—came down again and the immortal Albert dropped another goal from a mark by one of his forwards. IC would evidently not do to have him anywhere in the home half, so operations were trans- ferred to the visitors quarters. Warrington played well and before the interval a fine piece of work by Ratcliffe was responsible for Harris getting over the line. Only a point divided the teams on changing ends.

The second half saw Warrington's star in the ascend. ant. In the first five minutes Fish placed a goal such as might have made A. G. envious, and then followed a long siege of the Hunslet line. Fish got through once and was a certain scorer had he not slipped in attempt- ing to double round Mitchell. Quickly the hall went across again, and after a failure or two Harris scored smartly and placed the issue beyond doubt. The rest of the game was keenly fought, especially by the Hunslet forwards, but they never looked like scoring again, and in fact, at times the visitors were hard put to it to keep Harris out. The right winger played a dashing game and with a little luck would have been credited with four tries instead of two.

It will be remembered that at Hunslet, the Warring- ton forwards were simply pushed about like so many children, and never gave a worse show in the history of the club. Something of the sort was expected on Saturday, for the home side were short of several good pushers The result was quite different however, and a smarter or more energetic exhibition of forward play has not been given by the Warrington tearn.f or seine time. New blood works wonders, and it was evident that every man has not been a trier of late. Saturday's eight quite held their own against the fine front rank of the Hunslet club, and to their efforts is the success of the backs due.

Saturday was a day on which things came off for Warrington. Though some of the best men were absent, the substitutes played at the top of their forml Ratcliffe deserves special mention for his really "classy, display at centre. I have not seen Harris so well looked after and with such good results for a long time. Centre is certainly the " A team skipper's proper position, and his claims as reserve man for this position must not be overlooked. Fish played well and drew a lot of the attention from Harris' which is rather an advantage, Hockenhall was the best half back on the field, and repeatedly gave evidence of sound tactics and skill. Bate was a failure, and will alwaEs be so unless her parts with the ball more speedily Na udiciously. The Hunslet backs were about on a par. None played badly and none caught the eye as stars. Even , " Albert's " skill was not very apparent if we except his goal-dropping abilities. Jackson was a better half than his Captain.

There was nothing very startling about Saturday's results in the Northern League, except that Batley piled up a big score against Swinton. As soon as the cup-ties come round the Batley men seem to get a fresh lease of life, which bodes ill for their opponents. Broughton Rangers easily defeated Oldham and thus added the championship of the South East Lancashire Competition to their trophies for this season. Hull broke their run of ill-luck by winning the last of their League games, Huddersfield being the victims.

' Strangest of all Saturday's results was the do eat of Widnes on their own ground by St Helens, who won a close game by three points to two points. "What Ho!" said the rude Warringtonians when the news came. This opens out fairly the South-West Lancashire Com- petition, and gives Warrington, in my opinion,the best chance of success. Widnes were crowing last week, and already counted the cup as theirs, but it will take them all their time to win the Lancashire Senior Competi- tion Championship now, seeing that Wigan are ahead for the first time. In the South-West Lancashire Com- petition Widnes play both Runcorn and Wigan away, and defeat by either club will make Warrington's 1 chances very bright.

A THEATRICAL FRACAS.

County Court Action at Warrington.

Peculiar Case.

An action in which Evelyn Cie,:le, an actress, sued Ernest Luck, pantomime proprietor, for £2O dart:Ares for assault and battery was concluded at the Warring. ton County Court on Saturday before his Honour Judge Bowen Rewlands, K.C. Mr Browne represented the plaintiff and Mr Steel conducted the deface. Plaiethre case was that she was engaged by the Brothers Luck as second boy in the pantomime "Sinhad the Sailor," but her part was subsequently changed to that of second girl. The company had pertormed at Gateshead, and were travelling on Sunday, February 16th to Warrington to appear at the Roval Court Theatre. Mr Deere, a gentleman who originally pro- duced the pantomime, but who had resigned his con- nection with the campany, was still at that time travelling about with them as plairtiff's Cuter, and ob- tained permisaion from the Br others Luck to travel on this train to Warrington at the three-quarter fare. From what was said en the station the Brothers Luck seemed to have changed their minds atoat letting him travel. When the train got to L iamside station a few miles south of Newcastle-era-Fyne there was a short stay to allow tue carriages of another company travelling in the same directiest to be joined to the train. Miss Clyde heard a row going on at one of the compart- ments, and found the Brotheis Luck arguiog with Mr Deere. They were shouting "tore him out "and " Dim out." They then appealed to the stationmaster, and be said that as there was room in the com- partment the gentleman had a right to travel so long as the passengers with him did not object. Toe passengers did not obj,ct. The father of the d - feudant said " pull him out. I will take the responsi- bility." Defendant ordered the stationmaster to pull him out. Miss Clyde+ got in the carriage and told them not to have any bother. Defendant pushed her on one side. She told him that he must not push her. e then took her by the arm and twisted it to such an extent that she screamed out. She afterwards told him he was a emceed, and he said he had net touched her. The stationmaster made a certain remark, and the defendant made a reply which would be the subject of an action for slander in the High Court of Justice. When the company reached Warrington Miss Clyde went to Dr. ;leery, and he attended to her arm. The arm required attention for the whole week. She had her arm up when sh 3 went to the theatre, vest the Brothers Luck were most insulting. Defendant said he was not going to have her arm in a sling. She had a most worrying time the whole week. She was now a mere walking dummy on the stage. They had taken every sonc and every word from her. She showed Dr Smith, who was called in by the Brothers Luck, the certiecate she had obtained from Dr Geary, and although she was ordered by the Brothers Luck to show her arm, Dr Smith would not of course examine her arm except in the presence of her medical man. Dr Geary certified that Miss Clyde was suffering from shock. HP prescribed for her and she had had to take medicine since she left Warrington. At the outeet, Mr Browne drew attention to what he described as a contempt of court. An avet of the defendant's company, he said, repeatedly went in and oer of the court on Tnursday, when the case was come unencA, and told the witnesses for the defence what had happened in court. His Honour said it was a very abominable thing. Mr Steel said ha did not know why this was dragged in to prejudice the case. Mr Browne said it was not so dragged in. His Honour observed that such conduct was a very improper proceeding altogether. He went on to say that the question of whether Mr D Cre had interferred with the ladies of this company and endeavoured to get them to j oin another company was not relevant and need not be touched up-n. It was also very difficult to see how Mr Leek was justified in pulling him out of the train. That was net material. Mr Steel said he wished to convince his Honour that there was no este ult upon the plaintiff: His Honraar : That is the whole point. Ernest Luck, the defendant, was then railed, and des- cribed the journey to Leamside. Arrived there he tald the station inapector that there was a gentlemen travel- ling in the train who had no right to do so. He laid he was travelling under false pretences and asked the inspector to turn him out. He said he weuld not. He (defendant) then went to the carriage and told him to come out sa he had no right to travel in the train.. Mr Deere said " Don't you touch me. Go sway."' He refused to come out, and he (defendant) said he would put him out. He reolied " Don't dare to touch me. I shall strike'you." He (defendant) then seized held of him. He took his right wrist in one hand and put his ethereround his neck. Miss Clyde then rushed in and put her arm round Deere's, and pushing him (defendant) away said "You shall noti take him." Mr Philip Luck name into the compartment, and was going behind him when Darts fell forward and Miss Clyde screamed " Oh, my arm" two or three times. He (defendant) bad not Put his hands upon Mies Clyde at all. He was quite sure he did not touch Miss Clyde. He did net see her in a fainting condition. At one stopping place she looked after her luggage and was talking to some of the chorus girls. At the first Performance at Warrington on Mon- day evening plaintiff had her hand in what seemed to be a sling. He told her he could not allow her to go on like that. She said she weeld do nothing of the kind, and that he would have to par for this. He said he would see about that, and added "Go and fetch the best doctor," Dr Smith was called in. He introduced the doctor to the plaintiff. Std/ale Browne: We say nethMee against the doctor. He acted with the greatest of eriq aette. Defendant described the interview 'with the doctor, and said that the plaintiff afrerwarda continued to attend rehearsals and bad played ever since. Be believed she missed ens rehearsal. With reeard to what had been said about cutting her part down, she was treate , exactly ae others were, including himself and his wife, because the pantomime was too long. Be had never shown any malice towards Miss Clyde. Mr Browne Then the whole thing is concocted. Defendant : That's my opinion. In further croes-examination defeadant said thelplain- tiff had no right in the compartment. Her name was on anorher compartment. Evidence in sawn wss given by Philip Luck, Mr ' and Mrs Ralphor, James Johnson aud Frank Percival Mr Percival and Miss Davies. Dr Smith was called and described kis first interview with the plaintiff. He said he was introduced in the usual way by Mr Luck. He afterwards egamined the plaintiff at Dr Geary's house. She took her clothes off without any assistance. He watched her closely, and there was no indieation of pain. He examined her right arm. There was no bruising or swelling tender- ness, or discoloration. If the shoulder joint had been sprained he wool i have expected to find seine discolora- tion. He worked her arm and she then complained of feeling pain. He merely put her arm in the same posi- tion as it was when the took her clothes off. If defendant had taken hold of bar with a severe grip he would have expected to find a bruiee. From the evidence for the defence he sheull think the injury could have been caused in the way described. He did not think it was a serious injury at all. Dr Geary advised rest and plaintiff did not take it. In those circumstances he thoneht the cure was a quick one. Mr Steel : Did you form the opinion that Miss Clyde was shamming ? Witness : I thought it was put on. Mr Browne : have you any reason to doubt Dr Geary'a word!? Witness : Net a bit. Mr Steel submitted that the injury was very trivial indeed, and that Miss Clyde had not suffer ,c 1 any pain Comparatively from what resulted in the train. Dr Smith formed an opinion that sho was shamming. He submitted that the damages were altogether excessive. Assuming there was an aseeult she was only ent t;ad to nominal damages. Re centended, however, teat no assault was committed. Plaintiff acted very unwisely in going to ties carriage. She had nn business there. He submitted that the story of the witnesses for the defence was more probable and natural than that of the plaintiff and her witnesses. His Honour said he could not doubt there was a great deal of excitement, and that defendant got hold of the plaintiff with a recoil which he never for a moment contemplated. and would have t 3en the last man in the world to have inflicted the iojury. He thought, however, the matter was a very striae one. !Mr Browne said that they did not know when the action was laid in the first instanc what the damage would be. They did not know for instance what the doctnets bill would be. He had not told them that they would not require further assistance. They did not ask for £2O. They would be extremely f colish to do so. He admitted it was too much. His Honour said he thought that a jedgment for the plaintiff for £5 54 inclusive of everything would be strictly just between both parties, and would compen- sate the, plaintiff for the direct result of the assault which he found was in fact committci upon her. He gave judgment for that amount with costa. There was a large attendance of the public in court and the judge's decision was received with applause.

DEATH OF mR. JOHN PLATT. It is with a regret vv•litch we feel will be shared by many townspeople, that we record the death of Mr John Platt, at his residence, Clifton Lodge, Llandudno, at 8.30 on Friday morniug. For some years past Mr Platt had been suffering from Dead disease, and a recent attack of influenza, hastened his death.

lie came to Warrington in 1868 and opened a factory in Lythgoe's-lane for velvet cutting. His father carried 411 a similar business elsewhere 4nd the Warrington establishment was opened under the name of James Platt and Son. Business rapidly increased and other branches were opened in several parts of the town. Five years ago, the business was amalgamated with that of the late Councillor Joseph Wilkinson, and the two concerns were carried on under the name of Messrs Platt, Wilkinson and Company. More recently still, the businesses had been joined with those of the Velvet Cutters Association. Owing to ill-health, Mr Platt found it desirable to withdraw himself from business cares. Prior to his removal to Llandudno he resided at the Mount, Walton. In politics. Mr Platt was a Lineral. In 1876, he was returned as a repre-eutative of the North East Ward on the Warrington Town Council, and was elected an Alderman in 1890 About this time he took up his residence at Llandudno, and he finally severed his clonection with thA Council in 1894.

THE CORONATION.

How Warrington will Commemorate • the Great Event.

Suggestions Approved.

It is evident that the people of Warrington intend to celebrate the Coronation in a manner befitting such a memorable event. Ample indication of the desire of the inhabitants that the festivities should be on a scale worthy of the town, was afforded at the largely attended and representative meeting which was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall Warrington on Wednesday last week at the request of the Mayor (Mr J Charl- ton Parr) to discuss the matter. The Mayor presided and amongst those present were : The Mayoress and Mr Roger Parr, C,C., the Rector (Rev Canon Willis, the Revs E C E Carleton, I E It Tarbuck, B Percival, Father Wilson and John Yonge, Aldermen Monks,Shaw Green and Hutchinson, Councillors Smethurst, John Roberts, James Evans, Baird, Rogers and Wilkinson, Major Buckton, Dr Fox, Messrs C White T Birchall, J W Ilayward,T Ashcroft,George Wallington, E Wailing- ton, J G Craik, C S Burgess, E Imson, F Stott, and the Town Clerk (Mr J Lyon Whittle). • . . .

, The Maras delivered a short introductory speech. He pointed out that they had no precedent to guide them in considering the matter. because he thought this weuld be the first Coronation that most of them had experienvd. Speaking for himself be did not remem- ber another. (Laughter.) Not only that, but the Cot- poration was non-3xistent at the time of the last Coronation, so there was absolutely no precedent and they must strike out a line for themselves. The recent Diamond Jubilee celebrations, however, would help to a certain extent to show th iai the different ways in which they might commemorate the Cerination, but the circumstances were so vastly different that they would have to be modified to a very con,iderable ex- tent. 11, took it teat their anxiety would be to c7le- brate the auspicious event in a mariner worthy of the King and Queea and of the loyalty and &written which tneydentertained for them,and also of the traditions, the honour,and dignity of the good old town of Warrington. Their first business would be t ascertain where and bow the money was to come from, because the cele- bration could not be adequately carried out without a considerable outlay. They must make up their minds , whether the contributions were to be supplemented by the Corporation,which he supposed would be a question ; for them to a,k the Council's e.msideradon of, and also how the subscriptions which would be voluntary were to be collected.

The TOWN GLEE( read a letter of apology for non- attendance from the Depui4-11ayor (Alderman Ruberts) who expr:-ssed his willingness to assist in the carrying out of any suggestions which might be approved by the mettmg. _

At the request of the Mayor,the Town Clerk informed the meeting ef what steps were being taken for the celebrations in other towns. He also reported that the followMg, suggestions had already been laid b .fore the Parliamentary Committee : —(1) The presenta ion of an illuminated address to the King ; (2) a public procession in which the Friendly Societies and the Trades and Labour Council and others be asked to ci-operate on the day of the coronation; (3) a procession of children to take place on the following day ; (4) a treat to the aged veer ; (5) a treat to the children ; (6) that commemoration medals be struck . .

(7) that 'ail Public buildings in the t )wn be decorated and illuminated ; (8) that N public dinner be held ; (9) that a statue of her late Majesty be obtained ; (10) that scholarship bs formed in the technical schuols for elementary sch dare Mr ASHCROFT asked if it would not be advisable for

===== 0002601_19020314_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
:he Town Council to take up .the matter .first.

The MAYOR said the Council were anxious to consult the wishes of the townspeople before taking any steos. I No doubt the Council would rise to the occasion and if the townspeople wished them to vote a sum of money for the festivities they would do so. The Tome CLERK said the suggestions with regard to the treat for the aged poor and a miblic dinner had been deferred for the present. The meeting unanimously approved of the proposal to present an illuminated address to the King. The second suggestion was then considered. Mr J. Emsusi said that as chairman of a very influen- tial committee of the Friendly Societies, he was deputed to attend the meeting and say they were quite in accord with the suggestion to held a united procession on the day of the Coronation. The committee repre- sented owe 5.000 members who would very probably t ace part in the procession. Mr F STOTT (secretary of the Trades and Labour Council) said that organization had not discussed the matter as they bad not held a monthly meeting since the subject was introduced. Eti, could not say definitely what the Council wculd do. Toe majority of the men connected with it were also members of the Friendly Societies and he was afraid that if the Friendly Societies ce-operated, the Trades Council would make a very poor show. The Council represented nearly 2,000 workmen and if they could get all the members of the Ciliated societies to join in the procession he thought the Friendly Societies would have to work hard to exceed their number. (Laughter). The MAYOR suggested that a joint committee of the Friendly Societies and Trades Council might be formed and consider the matter with advantage. Mr STOTT said he would bring the suggestion before the Council. The meeting decided to approve of the suggestion to hold a Friendly Societies and Trades procession on th day of the Coronation, and also a children's proces skin and treat on the following day. The Mayor said they would all wish the children to enjoy themselves and no doubt they would be very glad to find funds for a treat. With regard to the commemoration medals the MAYOR said it seemed a very small thing to present each chili with a medal, but it would cost somethme like £l5O. Mr CRAM said that on the occasion of the Diamond Jubilee the medals were presented by each school. The TOWN CLERK thought the Corporation had a special die struck. Mr BURGESS : That medal was not given to all the children in the town. It was decided to leave the matter in the hands of the Committee. who would act as funds permitted. The Town Curnic said that on the occasion of the Jubilee in 1887, £739 14s id was received in subselp- dons, and at the Diamond Jubilee £754 was received. In referring no the suggestion regarding the docora Vow' and illuminations, the Town CLERK said the Par liamentary Committee suggested that the Town Hall Walker Fountain, ;Park Gates, Technical Schools Queen's Gardens, new Police Station. Museum, &hoc) of Art and the front of the Market Hall should be decora ted and illuminated. That might be done out of th rates and not chaTed upon the funds. Mei e had been no opportunity of calling a meeting of the Kectricity Commi'itee to consider the matter but he had no doubt that the committee would favour the proposal. The TowN CLERK said that the gas for the illuminations would be supplied at the rate of Is 1.1 per 1,000 feet. The suggestion was approved. The MAYOR said that if they had funds to meet the requirements of a permanent memorial such a memorial he felt sure, would be evr-mely desirable. It might be said that they had to "cut their coat according to their cloth " and be quite agreed with that, but still he thought they should have in ttn-Ir minds that a perman- ent memorial should be erected, so as to encourage people to subscribe and do something that would be an embellishment to the town. With regard to the sug- gestion that the memorial should be to the memory of the late Queen he said they all felt the deepest respect for her late Ma esty and the memorial would be ex- tremely acceptable. Lymm and .the Coronation.

A. meeting convened by the Urban District Conncil was held in the:Parochial Schools, pepper' street, on Tuesday night, to consider what form the .local commemoration of the Coronation should take. Mr W A Dewhurst, chairman of the council, presided.—The Chairman said the District Council felt it wa, quive time that the first steps were taken to arrange their procedure for the Coro- nation Day. He asked the ratepayers to help theca both practically and financially, because as theY perhaps were aware, at the present time they had no power to spend any money from the rates fa any purpose of that kind.—On the suggestion of Mr Kidson, seconded by the Rev G B Thurston, it ws9 decided to form a preliminary committee consisting of the members of the District Council and three representatives from each of the four wards, with Mr Dewhurst as chairman, to make:inquiries and t° report to a further meeting of the ratepayers.—Mr Mullard and Mr Moffat were appointed joint secre- taries, and Mr W A Lewhurst Mon treasurer.—All Eidson suggested the establishment of a swimming bath, to commemorate the Coronation. (Hear,hear.) He thought they ought to have something of a use' ful character, and not merely a memorial such as monument.—The Rev F C Bozman seconded Oa! proposition. He said there was a suitable piece 01 ground for a bath between the New Hall and tbe Conservative Club. A swimming bath would be educational to the youths of the district, and it was very important they should learn to swim."' Mr Watkin thought a public play ground for the poor children would be a suitable memorial. (Heat; hear.) A play ground would keep the children the streets.—The Rev & B Thurston seconded tbe proposition, and said there was a suitable field abutting between Statham and Newfield, belon¢ing to Mr Fairclough. if it could be purchased or rent- ed.—lt was decided to hold a meeting of. the committee on the 18th inst.

The Cost of Pauper Lunatics. At the Lancaster Board of Guar diens meeting on Saturday, a circular letter was received from Mr .1. P Muspratt, clerk of the Asylums Board,intimating that on and after April Ist the charge for mainten- ance of pauper lunatics would be increased from 9s4d per week to 9s 11d. The Chairman (Mr B B Dawson) said when the county council was established pauper lunatics were maintained in the Lancaster Asylum at 7s 7d per week. They were of the opinion at the time that the joining of the asylums for ad- ministrative purposesfrould wind up the cost at Lancaster. In the south of the county they did not care what they spent, and the South Lancashire members now dominated the Asylums BOard. A Warripgton Patent Dispute. Mr Justice Buckley had before him Vast week, in the Chancery Division, a petition by Joseph Crosfield and Sons. Limited, of Bank Quay Works, Warrington,and another for the revocation of letters patent granted to Ernest George Scott in 1897 for improvements in the concentration and distillation of glycerine. Mr ncott, who was for many years consulting chemist and engineer to Messrs Lever Bros, the well-known soap manufacturers, invent- ed long before the date of the patent now in ques- tion, the process for the distillation of glycerine, which was now used by 90 per cent of the glycerine manufacturers in this country. By that process three per cent at least of the glycerine was always Wasted. Mr Scott's patent of 1897 waS for the introduction of a second condenser, by means of which be got practically the whole of the gly- cerine. The petitioners alleged the invention was , in use at their works at the date of the patent, and they had obtained the idea from drawings of Studer, the second petitioner, made in 1895. They also alleged that Mr Ecott had :obtained his idea from their works, to which he had access, and that the invention was insufficient.—Mr Lewis Fdiuunds, appeared for the peti- tKio.enerasil, and Mr DMarviW aa Walton and Mr Gray for the respondent.—Mr Scott was called in support of his patent, and denied that he obtained the idea for his invention from designs made in 1895 in the Petitioners' Possession, or that from the autumn of 1894 until 1896 he saw any of Crosfield's refining or concentration plant.—Ris Lordship found that neither the process used nor the result obtained by the petitioners was the same as that of the respondent's patent, and dismissed the petition with costs.

MR. NORBURY WILLIAMS AND THE" EXAMINER."

A Withdrawal of Insinuations Made at Blackpool.

In the course of his speech at Parr Hall on Thurs• day last week. Mr Norbury Williams made two references to the comments he had passed on the Warrington press at a Blackpool meeting. His , withdrawal was in response to a letter from the editor or this paper and his remarks were as fol- low: I Ant to say something about what I said in Blackpool about your newspapers here. I want to set myself right on that point. gave an indication in Blackpool that in my:judgment a couple of your newspapers here were under the control of mem- bers of the Council. Now I have been informed it is not 60. I have had a letter from the editor of one of these papers in which he says it is not at all correct. I absolutely accept that gentleman's I word and I am delighted to hear it. I was mistaken in believing it—a quite natural belief I think—for the paper has been hammer and tongs against us in Warrington here, but I absolutely accept the gentleman's statement he has made in writing that there is no correctness in the suggestion that his 1 paper is under the control of any gentlemen in the 1 Corporation. So I accept it that no paper in War- 1 rington is under municipal control. I wanted to 1 say that in justice to myself but chiefly in justice 1 to this newspaper. Later Mr Norbury Williams said : I have been handed a letter from a gentleman in the audience connected with the local paper called the " Ex- aminer." He seems to think it is not quite plain that I have referred to his paper and said it is not controlled by anyone in the Council. I thought I said that. I thought it applied to every paper in 1 Warrington. If the gentleman from 0 the Exam- iner" wants me to say it more explicitly I say I 1 accept his word, the "Observer's" word, and the Guardian's" word and the word of anyone else who has got a paper in Warrington that they are in no way controlled by anyone in the Council. I am very pleased and I congratulate Warrington on having a press which is in no way connected with the Council.

THE WAR.

War Medals for Ambulance Men. Nine war medals for members of the Warrington Corps of the St. John Ambulance Brigade have been received from No. 4 district headquarters. They are for the following members of the corps —T Broornhall, G Buckley, J T Dimelow, El Galloway, IV Johnson, G Kelly, J Maddock, C J anseur, anct Ei T Arrangements are being made for a pubtio presentation of the medals, It will be remembered that Messrs Holloway and Newns, who were in South Africa with the Duke of Portland's contingent, received their medals from Lord Roberts at Welbeck Abbey.

Death of a Warrington Sergeant.

Sergeant J Croasdale,of the lst Manchester Regiment, recently died of enteric fever, while on active service in South Africa. Crcasdale vas a time-expired soldier when the war broke out, and was employed as parcel potter at Warrington Central Station. When the War Office authorities called for time-'xpired men, he en- listed for service at home, and afterwards volunteered for service abroad. His death is much regretted by all who knew him, and the large muster of railway men who attended • special service at St. Peter's Church en Sunday morning was a fitting testimony to the regard in which the late sergeant was held. A procession Was formed at the Central Station and headed by the C.L.C. hand marched to the church. The service was of a very interesting character, appropriate hymns being eung and the curate, Rev. Mr James, preached a thoughtful sermon on " Duty.' Mr Webster, was at the organ and very effectively played the Dead March in "Saul," the congregation meanwhile standing. After the service, the men marched back to the station headed by the band, which was under the direction of Mr T. Meredith.

Captain Crosfield's Departure for the Front.

Mounted Infantry: Companis " Send-off."

On Monday night Captain Gecrge R Crosfield left Warriegton for Aldershot to take command of a squad- ron of the and Imperial Yeomanry, which will shortly proceed to South Africa. It was fitting that the mem- bers of the Mounted Infantry Company of the local Volunteer Battalion, of which Captain Crosfield bee 'held command, should unite in according him a hearty "send-off," and with this object a " smoker" was held at the Patten Arms Hotel on Monday evening. Captain Crosfield's many good qualities, to say nothing of his previous record of ser- vice in South Africa, have won for him the hearty esteem and respect of every member of the company, and there was an exceedingly good muster to attend the " smoker." Coloer-Sergeant Connor presided. The (Aileen who attended with Captain Crosfield were Major Buckton. Captain Appleton, Captain Smith, Captain Dutton Fsirclough, and Lieutenatti Avery, Williamson, WilCoCks, and Potter. Amongst others present were Sergeant-Major Rawlinson, Sergeant-Instructors Ingram and Connor, Sergeants Buller:, Carter and Richardson, Farrier-Sergeant Ferguson and Sergeant Connor (trans- port section). A capital entertainment had been arranged, and all the items of a long programme were received with cordial appreciation. Those who gave their services were Messrs Kendrick, F Stringer, W H Ireland, L Aukland, H Mosley, F Fieldhouse, J Emson, and E Sanders, Lieutenant Avery,Lance-Corporal Dain- tith, and Privates Finch, Moores and Starkey. Mr Kendrick and Captain Smith accompanied. During a short interval in the proceedings a few speeches were made. Colour-Sergeant Connor said be had been deputed on behalf of the company to t(II Captain Crosfield how keenly they regretted his leaving them if even it was only tor a abort time. The presence on that occasion of so many mounted infantry men would tell him better than any words of his what was in their hearts. They wished him Godspeed and good luck, and asked him to come back and again be their captain. (Cheers.) Maj )r Buckton said that Captain Crosfield was going out for a second time and that meant a good deal. (Cheer.) Be believed that had it not been for his unfortunate illness they would not have seen him back until the war was over. His keenness for soldiering was such that nothing less than the positive commands of the doctor would have brought him home. • The men of Captain Crosfield's company in the Imperial Yeomanry were very lucky in having such a com- mander, and they of the Warrington battalion were very lucky in having had tim to form their company and command it until now. (Cl:rem) There was no better proof of the SUCCORS of his efforts than the ccrefuct of the company in camp latlt year. He watched them there,and while noting their efficiency and general good conduct. aiso came to the conclusion that Captain Crasfield was the right man in the right place. (Cheers.) Major Suckton then read the follow- ing letter from Colonel Ridgway :—" Colonel Ridgway is very sorry that a previous engagement will prevent him accepting the invitation to the smoking concert of the Mounted Infantry, as be wculd have very much wished to have assisted in a hearty farewell to Captain Crosfield, who had done so much for the interest and good feeling that has prevailed in the Mounted Infantry Company from its commencement. Colonel EidgwaY feels sure that in South Africa. Captain Crosfield will again show his mettle, and hopes that on his return he ' will again take up the duties of the company with ills • old comrades." (Cheers.) The health of Captain Crosfield was then heartily drunk. Captain CROSEIELD, who Was received with ereat en- tbaeissm and the singing of "For he's a jolly good
