SUNDAY TRADING.

warrington Free Church Council's

Action.

The above Council has issued a circular on the subject of Sunday trading which is worded as fol- lows Sir,—At a recent meeting of the Executive of this Council, a report was presented, by a Sub- Committee specially appointed to make inquiries, upon the existence cf Sunday trading in the town and neighbourhood. It was represented that this evil exists in very large and growing degree and the hxecutive therefore feel it to be their duty to call the attention of our Churches and schools to this very serious fact. They do not deem it desirable to suggest any legal steps toward its suppres- sion, even if such are possible. To take that course; might appear a harsh 'and arbitrary attempt to enforce Sabbath Observance, and would probably awaken more prejudice than sympathy. But it does, in their opinion, seem most desirable that the leaders of churches and schools, and the members of congregations generally, should do everything in their power to curtail this growing evil. The opening of shops for the sale of tobacco, &c, newspapers, sweets,fruit,and other such things, and of photographic studios, on Sunday, cannot be necessary, and in many instances temptation is thereby placed in the way of people generally, and of children and young persons especially to forget the sacred character of the Day of rest. The Committee would also point out that the opening of these places on Sunday is un- fair to tradespeople, who, either from religious motives or the desire for one day's rest in seven, close their places of business on that day. Unless the practice be checked by the wide expression of an adverse public opinion, there may be seen a still wider developement of this Sunday Trading than already exists. It is therefore respectfully suggested that ministers and preachers in our churches and missions should call the attention of congregations to this important matter, and also that Sunday school superintendents should urge their scholars to refrain from any such Sunday Trading themselves, and to discourage it in others. The Committee will be glad to find that their sug- gestion is sympathetically received, and that effect is given to it at as early a date as may be conven- ient. We are, on behalf of the Executive, Charles E Parker, president; C Hale Hocken, E Pierce Williams, Secretaries. Warrington, March, 1902.

Superintendent Higginbotham's Retirement.

An interesting ceremony was performed at the Assembly Rooms, Leigh, on Thursday when ex-Superintendent Higginbotham was made the recipient of several handsome presents as tokens of the esteem in which he was held during his con- nection with the County Police Force. Mr Higgin- botham has been a member of the Lancashire County Police Force for forty-one years, and for the last eleven years has been superin- tendent of the Warrington Division, which has its headquarters at Leigh. He retired on a well-earned pension last November, and was suc- ceeded by superintendent Cocker, of the Wigan District. A committee of local townspeople was formed with the object of raising by public sub- scription a sufficient sum to give Mr Higginbotham a handsome testimonial upon his retirement, and , over £2OO was realised. The committee decided to make him a presentation of a beautiful ornamental timepiece and a purse containing one hundred guineas, and to give .slrs Higginbotham a solid silver tea service, both of which have been suit- ably engraved and manufactured by Messrs Elkington, of Liverpool. The officers and constables of the Warrington Division have also subscribed for a smoker's cabinet, case of pipes, and silver matchbox, as a token of the respect in which th4y held their superintendent during the years he was connected with them. There was a good attendance of the members of the irolice force and the general public, and the gather- ing was presided over by Alderman Pennington, who was supported on the platform by Captain T E Witbington, JP., Mrs and Miss Withington, Superintendent Cocker, Councillor Eckersley, Mr J C Calvert, clerk to the magistrates, &c. Superin- tendent Higginbotham was also presented with a case of pipes from the Press of the Newton and Earlestown District of the division. Xetters to tbe Ebitor.

Aiderman Monks and the War- rington Teachers.

Sir,—Tbe remarks made by Alderman Monks, when the minutes of the Museum and Tectinical Instruction Committee came before the Council on Tuesday last have occasioned some surprise and n 3 little regret amongst the teachers of Warlington. The request, preferred by the Warrington Teachers' As3ociation, was that one of their number should be co-opted as a member of the Museum Committee. This. to most people, would appear a very reasonable request indeed. The teachers in this question are perfectly disin- terested. They have no axe to grind, but, having been eagaged in purely educational work all their lives, claim to possess that expert knowledge which should prove of great value in all matters appertaining to education. Surely Alderman Monks does not claim that all, or even most of the members of the Museum Committee are educational experts ? The Corporation are at present engaged in promoting an Education Bid. Whence comes this sudden desire to control the , educational affairs of the Borough ? Neither political party has, in municipal elections, laid any particular atress upon such questions. In fact, many councillors have shown themselves averse to serv- ing upon the School Attendance Committee—a Com- mittee whose work is most esseatial and important. Who is it then who is " pulling the strings " of the new Bill, and for what reason? If the Council are sincere in their desire to promote the best interests of education, they will find the teachers, from no ulterior motives, ready and willing to co-operate w.th them.—Yours truly,

TEACHER.

Sir,—Alderman Monks is reported to have said in reply to Councillor Smethurst that" He did not see that they (the teachers of Warrington)' had any right to expect to be elected on this committee in this sort of was. It would be a bad principle to permit any outside body to nominate represents- , dyes to seats in the control and gift of the Corpora- tion." The Teachers' Association did not seek to nominate a representati ve,but respectfully requested the members of the Museum and Technical Com- mittee to co-opt a teacher in the place of the late Mr Peers. But will Alderman Monks kindly point out where the badness of the principle comes in of seeking representation on this committee? We had an idea (mistaken apparently) that this committee existed not only to do good work but the best possible work for the town. We also thought (again mistakenly) that as a most import- ant part of the work of this committee deals with education the committee would welcome the pre- sence of the educational expert. The Warrington Teachers' Association consists of almost all the certificated teachers in Warrington. Many of these are engaged daily in the teaching of science, mathematics, and languages in addition to the ordinary work of the day school. Some of them have taught in science schools. It is a part of their duty to have at their fingers' ends Educa- tion Codes, Government Instructions, Science and Art Directories. They have passed through a long and arduous course of preparation and training. Many of them have had years of experience. Yet these men and womenlare to be denied any repre- sentation on a committee largely dealing with con- tinuation schools, technical,science and art classes. We make no attack upon the committee as at present constituted. We do not seek " seats in the coutroland gift of the Corporation " from personal motives. We wish as good citizens to put our ex- perience and knowledge of educational matters at the disposal of our fellow citizens. We confidently believe that they will say our request is an eminent- ly reasonable one.—Yours truly, JOHN UPSON,

Warrington Ragged School and Mission.

Sir,—The Committee of the above Institution earnestly appeals for help towards the purpose it is working for—the care and early training of War- rington's poorest children. Money, food, clothing, and workers too are wanted. _

The past severelwinter, and the cqpsequent uncer- tain employment for outdoor workers, caused addi- tional privations for the children. Lack of warmth, scanty clothing, in sufficient food, how keenly many of these little ones suffer during a protracted period of frost and snow, is only known to those who have been amongst them in the places were they live, or rather, exist. A greater demand than usual has been made upon the resources of the Mission. Thanks to the generosity of practical sympathisers, a number of children have been fre- quently supplied with a substantial meal, and pro- vided with clothing ; they have also had instruc- tion and amusement at intervals. We have to thank, amongst others, Messrs Strange and Son,and Mr J Horton for bread and cakes ; " A Friend " for clothing; Mr H3l Ashton for fuel. (These are re- cent donations.) The subscriptions received have enabled thejcommittee to purchase a large quantity of clothing, boots, clogs, &c, and these have been carefully distributed. We thank those who have helped in the past and hope to have their continued kind support in the future. We ask all who have looked upon and pitied the condition of these poor children, to assist us to reach as many as we can, to help us to influence their young lives for good. Almost any kind of donation is acceptable, for some of these wretched little ones are at times en- tirely destitute. Anything sent will, in every case. be acknowledged, and shown in detail in the annual report, a copy of which will be sent to each donor, We beg to inform those who prefer to help other- wise than by gifts of money, that there are arrange- ments at the Mission room in Upper Bank-street, for cooking suitable food, on a large scale, and that clothing is always distributed judiciously, and the recipients kept in touch with. Mr Robert Davies, whose death we all deplore, I was a true friend to the poor people of Warrington, and by them he will be greatly missed. I have per- mission to publish the following characteristic letter, received only a fortnight before his death.

Birch Dale, Stockton Beath, Warrington. February 11th, 1902, My dear Sir,—l am now in my 76th year. and much troubled from my heart, but the good work et the Ragged School is (amongst many other calla upon me this year) one that I must not neglect. I enclose you a cheque for a guinea and remain.— Yours truly, ROBERT DAVIES.

Mr J W Howarth. JAMES SBELLAND, (Superintendent). JOHN W HOWARTH, (Financial Secretary).

TEMPERANCE REFORM. Mr. Sam Woods and the Government Bill.

Address to Rechabites at Warrington.

Mr SAM WOODS, the labour candidate for Newton I division' presided at a public meeting aud concert held in Brick-street schools, Warrington, on Satur- day evening, under the auspices ot the Charity 1, Tent of the Warrington District of the Independent Order of Rechabites. There was a large attendance of members of the Order and their friends and the gathering was marked with gratifying success. Prior to 'the meeting a capital tea was partaken of and was moch enjoyed. The meeting commenced with the singing of the well known ode " Each effort to redeem our race " and afterwards a piano- forte solo "Sabbath evening chimes" was pleas- ingly rendered by Mr Josepe 11111. Miss Cowell gave a sweet interpretation oft' The Toilers and ! was accorded hearty applause. The CHAIRMAN then addressed the meeting, and , in the course of an able speech said the Rechabites took a principal part in the temperance movement of the country. There could not be a more elevat- ing or humane object than theirs, and blessings would result on every hand if they could induce every man and woman, toy and girl in the kingdom to become a member of the Society. He had always slooked upon strong drink as being the arch enemy of our national progress, and the greatest barrier to the progress of the working claseee in particular. Where there was much drink- ' ing there was very little thinking. If they could only succeed that night in securing some more adhesions to the policy they believed in they should have had a very successful meeting. He took , it that the Rechabites had four objects—in the first place the extension of temperance principles, in the second place the encouragement of thrift, in the third place, education, and in the fourth place,securing as far as possible a scheme of ' old age pensions. Did they ever think what the drink policy was? He was told there was invested in the manufacture and sale of drink in this coun- try nearly £300,009,000. They might take that money and place one sovereign against another, if they had land to put it upon, the whole of the dis- tance between England and .New Yorke—three thousand miles, and then they would have some left. Or they might take the amount of money used and spent in the manufacture and sale of drink and put a sovereign on every letter of the Bible, and they would have some left when they had covered every letter. This, however, was not the worst aspect. They must think of what the expenditure of that money produced. When they looked abroad in our towns and cities at the poverty, destitution, and crime resulting from drink, he thought it was time that tee people's eyes were opened_to a better cause and a better purpose in life. The Rechabites also encouraged thrift. They encour- aged a fair day's work for a fair day's wage, and, when a man had earned money, the economical spending of it and the putting of a little by for old age and infirmity. The next purpose which the Order had in view was education and there was no question agitating this country so much at the pre- sent time as education. What he said was that the temperance mosement had a great work before it in education—education in temperance principles. There were a great many questions in connection with the temperance movement, and ha had always thought that one of the greatest dangers, and one of the greatest curses was the tied-house system. He was very pleased to read the other day of a case tried in one of eur courts ins which the judge held that under certain circumstance- there could be no such thing as a tied house. Continu 1 ing the speaker said the brewer, in order to get hold Of a very ordinary public house, would spend a fabulous sum of money, and what was the result? Be would dispense to the publican simple and sheer poison for the people to drink, adulterated to the highest pitch. Not 'only that, but the publican in a tied-house was very little batter in nine cases out of ten than a caretaker. The brewer was the arch enemy of the temperance cause, and he was pleased to see from cases which were sometimes tried in the Law Courts and from others now engaging public attention that the time Was not very far off,—if they could only get more democratic Houses of Parliament,—w hen the tied-houEe system,which was such an abominable curse would be abolished altogether. They would then have removed one of the obstacles to the work of temperaace reform. (Applause.) Be had bad the pleasure to vote three or four times for the Local Veto Bill, which was the great craze of the age a few years ago, but had now gone into oblivion. Be ap- proved of that Bid, because it put the power :of regula- ting the Oriatt traffic late ths hands of the people,aud because he thought it would have a tendency to stern the flood of intemperance. But the bill had its blots, and he was not (elite sure, even if it had been passed, whether it would have been as successful as many an ardent temperance reformer thought it would be. He believed it would be difficult in many places to get twc- thirds of the people to vets for the closing of public' houses, because they would be considered to be doing an injustice to the publican by putting hiwo out cf busi- ness or out of work.lThis would lead to great irregularity and the Bill would not be so beneficial as some of the reformers would like. He would stop every public house in the country if he could ; but they must look at things from every point cf view. He had no practi- cal knowledge of club life in this country, but he understood that many were very little better than public houses. (Hear, hear.) He was very pleased to see that a resolution was going to be submitted to the meeting in favour of the Govern- ment:temperance Bill, He bed that tne;temeerance party throughout the country approved of the proposals of Mr Ritchie. the Horne Secretary, who introduced the Bill the other slay. Of course the Government was prac- tically pledeed to some scheme of temperance reform. The Bill.however,would in his opinion be of the greatest possible value, as a means of reducing the temptation to drink and it would have a very beneficial effect if it could only be passed into law.. The Bill was a very far- reaching one, and if it could only pass the House of Commons and the House of Lords and be put upon the statute book, temperance reformers would have some compensation for their arduous labours. There were, however, very few bills which went into the House of Commons sybich came out as they were brought in. They were manipulated and amended, and when they passed their third reading one could d scarcelythath t e Governmentknow w 'a cb wwearse taheori earnestginal aboutclauses.th a 0 lime . oneclause provided that if a • With regard to tee details. man had got an inebriate wife he could get a judicial separation, and if a woman had an inebriate husband who neglected her, she could also get a separation. If a Man got drunk in the street with 'a child under seven years of age under his care, he could he taken to prison without a sum- mons or warrant, and if a publican was known to sell drink to a man who was drunk he would havo his licence endorsed for the first offence aad would be liable to a penalty of £5O for a second offence. There were provisions in the Bill dealing with the clubs to which he had referred, to the effect that the same regulations should apply to them as far as hours !of closing 4,-.r c mcerned :isi appliedi to the 7publichouses. (Applause.) There was also a provision that the holders of grocers' licences would have to Show annually before mac'dstrates that they were justified in having them renewed, in the same manner that a publi- can had to. Grocers' licences had done an incalculable amount of mischief in the localities where they exiEteri , and bethought they should be pat under the same rigid discipline and government as the public houses. The I tern perarce question was fraught with diffioulties and , be hoped that Mr Ritchie, who he knew to be a man of rnetal,in fact be was his '(the speaker's) ideal member of the present Cabinet, would be firm. He knew Mr ' Ritchie to be a strong minded man and he had very great faith in him. In conclusion he asked his hearers to look ' at the sad results of the drink evil, at the innumer- ab'e temptations held out for the encouragement of . drink, and at the amount of crime which was , due to it. Even Mr Ritchie, he held, stated in the House of Commons that over 50 per cent at the cases that came under his notice were (be direct cause of drink. Surely if they had an atom of human nature in their veins, to .say nothing about Christianity, it was

their duty after observing the dire effects of the drink evil in our land, Co rise up as one cnan end say " The time shall soon come when these temptations shall be removed and the drinking system discouraged, and when our people shall be sober and thrifty from one end of the land to the other.' (Applause). The entertainment was then proceeded with. Mr A Griffiths delighted his hearers with an effective rendi- tion of " Mona" and was vigorously encored. The recitation "The factory chimney" was feelingly given by Mr G Whitfield and a duet " Very suspicious" by Miss Cowell and Mr Nichols was well received. _ _

An address was delivered at this stage by Mr R C Griffin, member of the Board of Directors of the Order. He said it struck him very forcibly what a splendid object lesson to young men they had in their Chairman. He was sorry Mr Woods had not his seat reserved in rarliament but there was someone else keeping it warm for him,and they should have the opportunity of seeing Mr Woods an M.P. betore very long. (Applause): Toe speaker proceeded to give some interesting details 29 to the working of the Order, which he said had passed its sixty-fifth anniversay. Ho thought they could say that they had stood the test of time and that the society was a sound and substantial one and woald do good to anyone who joined it. gnat was the ' first organization of its kind that Wa3 established purely for total abstainers. It was an interesting fact that the members experienced only from seven to eight days of sickness per annum, as compared with eleven or twelve days in other societies. He knew of no better Friendly Sochty in existence at tho present time. They had registered snore branch- s than all the other societies put together for fourteen years in succession, and during the past twelve months they had opened e'eity five new adult tents and eighty f nir new juvenile tents, making an aggregate of one hundred and sixty nine branches which they tIA been atie to es!ab- lish and place in working order. The temperance movement never occupied such a healthy and strong position as it did at the present time and he should be mistaken if they did not send thar chairman to Parlia- ment to push on the measures which they were so anxious to pass. The progress of the Order of Recha- bites han been most marked. In the yearlllB7l there were 23,219 members ;in 1381, 54,221 members ; in 1891, 146,033 members, and at the commencement of 1901, 269,017 members. From the returns which had already been received he hoped they w'ould be able to show au increase for last year of over 10,000 members. (Applause). They had in their juvenile order 101.000 members, who were becoming soldiers of tempesanee. They had only 291 deaths in 1890 out of those 104,000 children. The death-rate for adult members in the Foresters was one death to every 83 members ; Cnid- fellows, one in 89 ; Rechabites, one death in 155. (Applause). The number of pledge-breakers in their Order was very 'small, being abnet one in 100. The total income for 1900 was £256,353, and the expendi- ture £207.743, having a saving on the year's working of £43613. Their accumulated funds amounted to £1,250,000. The speaker urged his hear ?.rti to do all in their power to advance the canse of temperance, and expressed the hope that the chairman would soon be re- turned to Parliament to help forward temperance legL- , lation. (Applause). , Miss Cowen then sang " The Holy City " very credit- ably, and Mr G Whitfield contributed the lanehable recitation " To morrow" in capital style. Mr A Griffiths was very successful in he interpretation of " Good Company" and was watmly applauded. The following resolution was moved by Bro. Wll !Sherburn :—Tuat this meeting of Charity Tent NI. 11, I Branch of Warrington District No. 4 of the Independent Order of Rechabites. while regretting that the Govern- meet has not introduced a more comprehensive and drastic measure of Temperance Reform, cordially we- cornea and approses the Bill introduced by the Horne Secretary to change the date of Brewster Sem- sions from August to February ;t prohibit clerks of licensing benches from having a financial interest in the grants of licences ; to prevent the issue of occasional licences in other than open court ; to give licensing , benches absolute control over grocers and other licen- sees to increase the powers of the police in regard to 'drunken persons, and especially those found into7cMated while in charge of infant children ; to restrain habitual drankardenncl ta neakelicencees responsible for drunk- enness on their premises ; to enable married persons to secure a separation order from an habitually drunken partner ; to register clubs ; and to enable the police to enter the same. That copies of the foregoing resolution be sent to Mr Ritchie, Mr Balfour, and tee Borough member, Mr R Pierpoint. (Siate 1), FAr Ross, C.R. Bro W WRIGHT (Juvenile Superintendent) seconded. Sunday Observance. Another Sermon by the Vicar of

Frodsham.

There was a crowded congregation at Overton Church on Sunday morning to hear the pronouncement the Rey 11 B Bing,:t had announced to make on the question of ; Sunday observance—a question, it will be remembered, : on which the rev gentleman dilated at length some days ago, his opinions creating no little sensation among his parishioners, and meeting, indesd, with a most vigor— ; ous opposition. His text was based on " The streets of the city shall be full of boys and girls playing in the streets thereof." After briefly sketching the history which gave rise to his text the preacia,r said it w.is contrary to God's will that the day of prayer and praise should be made gloomy and devoid of gladness. It was with the: object of emphasising this truth that Sunday should be bright and gladsome that he spoke so strongly on the question of Sunday observance a few days ago at tire Rural Con-; fereuce. He had been asked by the Rural Dean to intro- duce the subject in order that there might be a discus- sion upon it. The meeting was not a public one, and was merely a small assembly of clergy and laymen. And before such an audience he felt be might express himself without fear of misconstruction. Had ho uttered a few platitudes on 1 the subject they would have been intensely doll and unprofitable and no discus4on wou)d have been possible. Etc knew reporters were present but it never occurred to him that his observations were of sufficient importance to be proclaimed throughout the length and breadth of the land as they had been done. Very few of the newspapers had. however done him the justice to state fully what he pad said. Most of them had simply announced that ti. 3 advocated Sunday sports and the introduction of the Continental Sunday. This was an entire perversioe. He simply advocated they should make Sunday a day of gladness to the young to be looked forward to with pleasure. It was b2tter for them to play games after Sanday School than to loaf about smoking pernicious cigarettes. Children should be treated as children and nest as grown-tip people. It was unwise to make Sunday. weary and dreary to children with the result that they grew up to hate it and the religion identified with it, In advocating this principle he was only following the exam pie of the saintly John Keble and the large hearted Charles Kingsley. With respect to Sunday golf this was limited ftrictly to those who could not play on any other day owing to confinement in places of business., They could well extend their indulgence to such new recreation being not only right but an absolute necessity for their bodily health. He had always spoken against Sunday golf playing at Frodstram by those who could play on any other day. Ho had also said that the clergy of the parish of Cheahire should combine in order to see whether the men in the parishes engaged in large works could not go to churn, and this suggestion was adopted and a committee formed. They bad abolished slavery in the colonies but it was not abolished in England so long as

men were compelled to work on Sunday. The well-to do classes with their Sunday luncheons were really ignoring their own spiritual welfare, while the increased fashion of travelling by rail on Sunday seemed to be done inpefiance of Gad's will. What had caused the most disquietude was his reference to the Continental Sunday whoa be said be preferred it to their own. It was true he had said it and he repeated it and it was an opinion shared by a great many people. In Italy the people went to the church in the morning sad spout tile re- mainder of the day most decorously. If theatres were onea in Paris it was only a single city in a single coun- try and it was unreasonable and unfair to take it as representative of the Continent. It there were races on Sunday in Paris there were races on week days in England. Surely if a thing was esseadally bid in itself it was bad every day in the week. Numberless evils sprang from horse racing as the Goodie case proved. If there were theatres in Paris open on Sunday there were gin palaces open in London and to whiela would they rather take a young child ? They should cast out the beam in their own eye before they plucked the mote out of their brothers. He had been charged with advocating the introduction of the Continental Sunday in England. That he deaied. Ile would quote the words he used, " Worship, rest and recreation should be toe three main features of tbe Lord's day. Don't let the clergy to afraid of introducing what is called the Continental Sunday. That is a worn out old bogy which insular ignorance has too often corajured up." His meaning was they should not no afraid of trying to make Sunday a happy day by the fear of in- troducing toe Coneineetal Sunday. In the wends of al leminent statement "What he had said he had said." He would not be dared from believing what he believed by 1 any amount of abuse car calumny and be had had a good I deal of it lonely. His only regret was that be had caused pain to same in his perish tor whom he had a strone regar lie was no advocate of sports on a Sunday bet be was an earnest advocate of reasonable health-giving recreation especially to children. In the evening there was again a crowded church. The vicar preached from Psalm cxviii., 24," This is the day whicio the Lord bath made ; we will rej >ice and be glad in it." Dealing with the question of recreation the rev gentlemen said that, of course, must depend on age and circumstances. Far the little child he could not think it wrong that is should he allowed to play tor an hour or two on Stldtlay. He believed it w iold give more willing attentim to religious teaching if it saw that they understood its nature, and were trying to make Sunday a bright well happy day. For older ones, young men and women, be quite saw that it would be daogereaa to recommend games of any kind, but recreation, a renew- ing of their bodily as well as of their spiritual vigour they needed wad should have. They might well urge them to refrain from games as being likely to absorb all their thoughts, and to prove a snare to themselves and others. And they might remind them that what might to beneficial for a Child WAS not neot333Arily SO for those who were no longer children. Toe Apostle said, " When I was a child I soalte use daild, I understood as a child, I thought as a child, but when I became a man I put away childish things." The best eecreati on for all people on Sunday afternoon was, in his opinion, to be in the open air as much as possible,and to be learning lessons from God's great book of nanure,conflrming those lessons they shonil'l have a:ready learnt in the morn- ing from his o [her great book cf grace. They desired that fathers and mothers should do what they could ter their own sakes,, as well as for their whildren'a, to observe Sunday as the Lord's day, and as a day in which to rejoice and be glad. So far as his own views were concerned he thought he might fairly point to what he had tried to do to inducephe men of this parish to spend part of the Lord's Day in worship. For several years, as they knew, apart from the ordinary services, he had condueted in that church a men's class on Sunday after• noon, and they had now on the roll as many as 87 mem- bers. In another part of the parish there was a similar (lase carried on by one of their lay readers—Mr Stokes. He asked then to be judged by what he had done. He pointed to his work, aid not merely t o what he had said. IF he might do so with all reverence, he would say as Christ said to his tradncere, If ye belie ye not me, believe the works." He was most ciesirons to uphold the religious character of Sunday, and to keep it as tee Lard's Day but he felt sure they (the clergy) would never succeed in doing so if they held themselves aloof from the people and merely preached at them on the subject. They needed to stand side by side with their fellow men, especially those of the poorer classen and to show them that they e eternal into aid understood their diffloultiec and temptations, and were willing to help them, so tar as they could, not merely with words but with deeds, t fle,lat with them and for them the great battle against the iniquitoes traffic in stronnedrink, to be their champions against the tyranny of capital. and to secure for them some time every Sunday for joining in public worship, for rest and recreation. They needed also to stoop to the level of children, and prove to them that they wanted to make Sunday a bright and happy day for them.

Reference at the Wesleyar't Chapel.

At the Trinity WeAlyan Chapel, Frodsha ra 0 a Sun- day morning, the Rev T Bailey (superintendent minister) preached on the subject .ot Sabbath observ- ance. Taking as his text Exodus xx he dealt with the Sabbath as being an institution of divine command and not an eccieaiastical arrangement. Ha explained the history of Sabhatism,especiaily as set forth in the fourth chapter of Hebrews. The Sabbath was holy, and, therefore, according t7i the root idea of holiness, as set fourth in the Scriptures, it was a day separated and claimed by God, and set apart for the benefit of man. It was not a day simply for intellectual and moral im- provement but for rest and religion. Its observance was a necessity on physical and spiritual grounds, for the individual and tide nation. It was God's special gift to the working man. The continental Sabbath was vigorously condemned by the preacher, and he pro- ceeded to axplaia how the Sabbath should be observed.

SHOCKINC FATALITY AT DITTON JUNCTICN. Runcorn wine Merchant Cut to Pieces.

A shocking\ fatality occurred at Ditton Junction. Widnes on Monday night. As an express from Crewe to Liverpool dashed through the station about eight o'clock, a man who was working Clote at hand beard a noise, and on going up the line he was horrified to find the mutilated retains of a man. He procured assistance, and the body was taken to the Blackburn Arms, and later identified as that of Mr Francis George Burns, Wine merchant, carry- ing on business at Widnes and Runcorn. It raised that deceased had travelled from Runcorn en route for St Helens, and he would have to change at Ditton, but no one appears to have seen him on the platform The body was found at the Liver- pool end of the station, and it would appear he had attempted to cross the line at that point. Sergeant-Major Burns was vary well known in St. Helens, and the news of his tragic end spread rapidly in the town this morning, expresSions of great regret being heard on all sides. On leaving t:de Royal Engineers as sergeant he became the sergeant-major of the St. Helens Engineer Volun- teers, and retired from his position as such on reaching the age limit a few years ago. Since that time he had been in business as a wine and spirit merchant, having establishments in et. Helens and Widnes. It is only a few months aim that Sergeant- Major Burns was seriously injured in a cycle acci- dent.

INQUEST AND vEaDicr. At the Blackburn Arms, Ditton, on Wednesday, Mr Brighouse held an inquest on the body. Mr Riley of St Helens, represented the relatives. Mary Ford, living in Harrison-road, Halewood, which, it was explained,was close by Ditton Station, said that deceased called at her house in a friendly way about seven o'clock on Monday evening, as he had a little time to spare. Ile said he was going to St Helens. He was perfectly sober, and seemed of a cheerful disposition. In answer to the coroner, Inspector Strickland said that nobody saw deceased about the station. The last to see him was Mrs Ford. Georg l Dodman, breaksadan on the London and North-Western Railway, stated that he was stand- ing near the express when it went by,but the night was very dark and he could see nothing. He heard a sort of thud, and thought the engine had burst a tube. Afterwards he found the bociy about sixty yards from toe end of the platform at the Liver- pool end of the station. Inspector Derbyshire ot the London and North- Western Railway, Edge-hill, said there were naarks on the engine when it reached Lime-street. Mr Riley elicited that the train due at Ditton at 7.59 from Manchester would travel into the station on the same line as the express went through on I and both he and the coroner were of opinion that the deceased,finding himself somewhere down No 1 plat- form, decided to cross the metals at the Liverpool I end, no doubt thinking if he saw the train that it was the 7 59 stopping train, and that he intended to go to the station by way of Liverpool. The pry returned a verdict of " Accidental death.'

£1,115 Subscribed. The Duties of an Elective Auditor.

Oa Monday afternom tn 9 subscribers to the Norbury Witliams Testimonial Fuad met in the A.couutants Hall, Manchester, and presented Mr Waliaaas with aa address, a sum of money and other articles, in recw,mi- tion of his work as a citizens' auditor. Tile Rev Canon Hicks presided.

Mr ICorter read several letters of apology for inability to be at the meeting. Mr t.4ehwann, M.P., who was to have mad the preientation. was d?tained in London.

Sir William Agnew very much regretted he could not be present on the °cession of the presentation to his eA- oelleat friend Mr Norbary Waka:ns. The Rev Dr Mc- Laren wrote regretting tiaat another engagement would prevent hi g being at the meeting. Mr Charles Thomp- son, Mr G D Kelley, Mr G B Birdsall, and others also wrote.

Mr Schwann, M.P„ extended Lis " most cordial greeting to the warrior upon whose brows you meet to bind the laurel." The Chairman said the work which Mr Williams hal done had had enormous results which bad been felt not only in Manchester but through every municipality and on every local bare in the country. This much he thought they might fairly claim—that the high character enjoyed at this time by the Mancueeter City Council for honesty and efficiency was due in no small degree to the watchful care with which the citizens' auditor for this city had discharged hi i laborious and responsible duties. (Rear, hear.) Mr Wiliams had been a man of combat. Woen people engaged in any kind of conflict they drew the eyes of other persons to them, and happy was the man who, like Mr alorbury Williams, had coo- ducted himself on go conspicuous a stage and yet had never allowed anyme to find a flaw in his personal charactar oc persoual honour. (applause). Mr Porter read the address which was to be presented to Mr Williams, together with a clock, a writing cabinet and a cheque for a considerable sum of money. In the address " the undersigned Manceester eitinns and friends " desired Mr William to accept the tokens " as some expression of our thanks for the great services you have rendered, first to your own city, and indirectly to every municipality in the kingdom. We recognise in you the faithful guardian of the public interest, the unswerving critic.: of municipal slackness, the enemy of public corruption in every form. Regard- less of political party, and braving at once the calumny of foes and the misunderstanding of friends, you have by your cousistent public action perceptibly raised the standard of municipal purity, and have embodied in your life the important principle that eternal vigilance is the indispensable condition of efficiency in local self-government. You have your reward in the I conviction of having worked for the public weal, and in finding yourself re-elected as citizens' auditor by in- creasing majorities. All these labours and conflicts yo-a have cheerfully undertaken without fee or reward,partly that your bands might he freer for your difficult and delicate task, and chiefly because the law, in providing for the remuneration of a citizms' auditor, makca no provision at all for the particular inquiries which you have undertaken, the only inquiries which vitally con- era the public welfare." Tha address was signed on behalf of the subscribers by Edward Lee Ricks, chair- man ; Robert Ramsbattorn, treasurer ; Alexander Porter, hon secretary, Manchester, 10th March, 1502. Mr W Simpson then presented the address aad the other tokens to Mr Williams.

Mr Williams, who was received with cheers, said that he had been so accustomed for twenty-three years of his life to an atmosphere of vituperation and fault. finding and blackening that after listening to the speeches that had lust been delivered he hardly knew where he was. He felt the force and truth of Mr Schwann's remark that he had not in his public work been actuated by a desire for notoriety. He had been drawn, like many other men, to fight for "admirable but unpopular causes " ; his sympathies went to " the cause that needs assistance and tte wrong that needs resistance,' arid it was a very happy experience to be so kindly received. He regrected that so many of his former friends had turned their backs upon him because of his pubic c induat, but he submitted that he had been .:imply acting in accordance with the 'view taken of a citizens' auditor's duties by the late Lord Chief Justice Russell. In the face of that ruling he thought no one would deny that an elective auditor should do more than look at vouchers and see the accounts correctly kept. The work of a professional auditor for the last 21 years had been done by the best firms in Manc'aester,and an idea of the magnitude of the work was galood by learning that the invoices for last year numbered 38,535. and the cheques 60,003, and that the turnover was £10,870,330. All the more needful, therefore, was it to have an auditor vrtur represented not the spenders but the money-finders. Mt Williams suggested that elective auditors should be found to do work for the nation. The remount scandal seemed, he thought,to po)at to tho need •for independent examination of Government accounts.

IL Wa9 announced that the total amount subscribed

was £1,115. The meeting closed with a vote of thanks to the etiairmaa.

ST HELENS.

ST, HELEN 3 NI7RSE'S FATAL OVERDOSE

- - The death of a nurse named Catherine Louisa Thomas of St. Helens, was investigated by the Bath coroner on Saturday. She bad just returned from a visit to her mother and brother, who is a solicitor at St. Helens. She was engaged to a doctor at Shrewsbury, and had told him she was in the habit of using chloroform against sleeplessness and neuralgia. On Thursday, having to go on night duty at the Bath Hospital, where she had been nine years, she went to bed at half-past five, and was subsequently diaeovered dead, her fade being buried in the clothes. There was a chloroform bottle in the bed. It was suggested that deceased might have taken sufficient to render herself unconscious, and then turned her nose and mouth mn the pillow, causing asphyxia. The Coroner said the facts did not conform in any way with the idea of self-destruction. The jury returned a verdict of " Misadventure through an ove dose of chloroform."

Fotto- nately there are few deaths, only one from scarlet fever, two from typhoid, three from measles, and one from diarthcer., a total of seven, a?.ainst six in the previous fort night. The general deathrate is 24 5, against 285 and 209 !wit year, and the birthrate has gone up from 36.4 to 433.

Force Houses, a colliery village Dear Sunderland, was on Monday the scene of a brutal murder. A man named Ernest Wood,on returning from work, attarksd his wife with a carving knife, cutting her throat right across. She cxpired M a few minutes. Wood next attacked a woman named Bruce,with whom he and his wife lodged, but fortunately sbe held up hew arms, and though wounded, escaped serioug injury. Wood then turned the 17.nife on himself and cut his own throat, but not fatally.

THE Rev. Frederick E. Paulley, curate of Holy Trinity Church, Portsea, lives at 73 St. Ronan's Road, East Southsea, and takes private pupils. "A few years ago," said the reverend gentleman, "I had to be extremely particular in my diet, as I suffered very much from INDIGESTION AND GENERAL DEBILITY. There were many kinds of food I had to avoid. I suffered very much then, and, having read in the daily press of the ex- cellent results in similar cases arising from persons taking Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, I determined to give them a fair trial. I obtained a box of the pills, and before I had taken the whole of the contents I found I was being bene- fited. I continued taking Dr. Williams Pink Pills regularly until I had finished three boxes, by which time I was completely cured. I have recommended Dr. Williams' Pink Pills to several people, with splendid result. I have known them to be very useful indeed in skin eruptions, and such-like complaints. " A little boy, one of my parishioners, had ABSCESSES #LL ROUND HIS NECK. On my recommendation, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills were ad- ministered, and in a few weeks he was all right, the abscesses having gradually disappeared. Another case was that of the grandmother of the boy referred to. She had suffered for a long time from a terrible wound on the leg, and the medical gentleman who attended her described the case as a serious one. I recommended to her Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. She took them 1r a 'ow weeks, and was quite cured."

Shocking Fatality near Warrington.—Haydock Man Killed on the Railway. On Sunday morning between six and seven o'clock a signalman found the body of John Greally Lime-street, Haydock, on the railway line. Life was extinct, the man apparently having been dead for several hours. The police were communicated with, and the body was removed to the Swan Inn, Winwick. There were several bruises on the man's body, the probable effect of having been caught by a passing train. Greally had been to Warrington on the previous night :and returned to Earlestown by the 10 30 p.m. train. He was after- wards seen in Earlestown with a friend, and it appeared as if Greally had set, out to walk to flay- dock under the impression that he was proceeding Along Messrs Ii Evans and Co's colliery railway, but he actually went War ringt on way along the London and Sorth-Western Railway, and was knocked down by a train, Deceased bad evidently been struck on the back and hurled some distance. After that, from marks on the permanent way, he seemed to have scrambled some distance and then died.
